

=== Introduction ===


--- Welcome: Course Objectives and Overview ---

Hello, my name is Jason Taylor. I help development
teams deliver project requirements faster
and with less risk by automating build and delivery processes.
In this course on the Jenkins
continuous integration build server
I will introduce you to the foundational
aspects of Jenkins and related concepts
using a step-by-step approach with the end result being
a complete and automated build system
all the way through deployment.
This course will feature Jenkins
on the Windows operating system,
but all the main principles and most of the demos
are equally applicable to other operating systems.
I will show operating specific
videos and screenshots when needed.
Being a bootcamp on Jenkins, there are just
four major objectives for this course.
First you will learn the key concepts
related to Jenkins and build automation;
this includes understanding how Jenkins
works and the benefits it provides.
Second we will build a complete end to end automated build
system starting off with our source code in GitHub.
Schedule compilation, quality analysis, artifact publishing,
and finally deploying our project to an application server.
Third, as we build the automated build system, you
will master the essential aspects of Jenkins.
This includes learning and understanding the
user interface and how Jenkins is designed.
How to create configure execute and maintain projects;
the maintenance and integration of other
tools and extending Jenkins using plugins;
and finally how to enable security and Jenkins
to ensure users see only their projects.
All three aspects above will provide you an excellent starting
point to apply projects or the ones that you support.
In order to accomplish the course objectives, we will
start off with the introduction to core concepts.
After a good foundation has been applied, we
will walk through the installation process,
including related tools and their configuration.
Once everything is set up and ready to go we
will dive into Jenkins with our first projects,
getting to know Jenkins along the way.
After an initial orientation in Jenkins, we'll cover
one of the most popular build workflows in Jenkins:
building mavenized Java projects.
A major component to the modern build system is
automated unit testing and code quality analysis.
We will hook up Jenkins to several
tools for testing in quality,
then use those tools to affect the
build status for our projects.
Once we are confident we have a good solid project build
we will configure Jenkins to publish those
artifacts for other systems to use,
and then deploy those artifacts
to a running application server.
We will finish things off by getting
to know security in Jenkins.

--- Course Focus and Approach ---

Some decisions had to be made to keep
this course as focused as possible.
This course is designed for those new to Jenkins.
I decided to record most of the videos on Windows since
that is the most common desktop operating system.
However, there will be lectures just for Linux and
Mac OS users when that distinction is important.
Since you can make Jenkins do just about anything I
had to narrow things to the Java technology stack,
using Maven as the build management tool
and deploying to the Apache Tomcat server.
While Jenkins supports many languages,
technology stacks, and deployment systems,
Jenkins really excels with Maven projects.
You should be able to apply the principles of this
course to just about any technology stack you need
For source control I decided to use Git,
and by extension the GitHub service,
since Git is currently the most popular
source control system available.
If you use another source control management tool that's ok;
Jenkins likely has a plugin to add support for that tool.
Finally, I decided on a very simple Jenkins set up.
While Jenkins can be highly distributed, I kept
things easy to understand and to follow along
by running everything on the same system, at least initially.
This allows for a simplified and complete build
system, all the way through project deployment.
Once we understand the basics on a simple
setup we can ramp things up a bit later on.
The approach I take in teaching this
course is to give just enough theory
to understand the critical concepts and what's going on.
This course starts with the core concepts,
which is a high level overview,
and each of those concepts are more and more reinforced
during the practical examples sections of this course.
Within those examples, or screen casting demos,
I will walk through each step, one at a time.
Additionally I provide the complete source
code for all the projects on GitHub
and a listing of commands used throughout the course.
All this is to keep the course focused and
compact, since I know many people have busy lives
Most of the videos are short and focused, so you can
jump directly to the topic you are most interested.
I provide links to more information and of
course resources within the lecture resources,
so check those out during the course.
With all that I want you to pause the
video lessons so you can do the examples,
or follow along in order to get the most out of this course.


=== Full Installation ===


--- Build Maturity ---

Welcome, in this lecture, we're going to
start the discussion about build maturity,
and then how to get to continuous integration.
Most projects start off with very little
in the way of a structured build process.
Perhaps relying upon a single developer's
integrated development environment, or IDE,
to generate the deployment artifact or resources
needed to be placed into production manually.
For all practical purposes there really isn't
that build process, or it's very manual.
This often leads to the dreaded it works on my machine
dilemma especially if it doesn't work in production.
The next level up would be what I would
consider a minimal build process.
It would involve the use of build tools, like
Maven, or Gradle, NPM, Grunt, Webpack, etc.
to generate a repeatable build process
that manages the project's dependencies,
compiles code, and generates a deployment artifact.
At this point teams would still be using a dedicated
developer, that is the build guy, for this purpose.
Of course this has many drawbacks, but at
least many manual steps have been automated.
If we take the minimal build process just described
and dedicate a standalone server for this process,
perhaps using a build server or orchestrator
like Jenkins to perform a build on demand,
or when new changes are detected in the project we can
free up the build guy for actual development work.
Then we can automate other tasks we would like to
do but just didn't have the time for previously,
like running unit tests and static code
analysis to ensure high code quality.
If we accomplish this much we now have
a continuous inspection build process.
A continuous inspection build process automatically
detects changes in the project source code repository,
performs a build on those changes, and
runs various code quality inspections,
normally unit tests and static code analysis.
Simply getting to continuous inspection is a huge step
forward in a project or company's build maturity.
However, there are still several more levels to go.
Many people actually use the term continuous
inspection and continuous integration interchangeably.
However, to me, in order really achieve true
continuous integration we need a branching strategy,
and an approach for integration of the
changes into the main development branch,
and a release policy for production.
Continuous integration includes
everything from continuous inspection
but adds a structure branching strategy
and a release management policy.
The automated building and running of unit tests and
code quality tools is just continuous inspection.
With a structured release management process
and agreed upon branching strategy in place,
we can firmly say CI stands for continuous integration.
As we will see in a moment, Jenkins can serve as a central
part of a robust continuous integration pipeline,
which is what we will focus on in this course.
In fact, the build maturity continuum doesn't stop here;
We can evolve things to do automated deployments
to test servers and prepare for production,
thus making it to continuous delivery.


=== The Basics ===


--- What is Jenkins? ---

Welcome, in this video, we're going to look at how
Jenkins works by reviewing the Jenkin's architecture
and getting to know the terminology used by Jenkins.
As with any software Jenkins has to run on a host system.
This system determines which installer is used
for Jenkins and the tools available for the use.
Jenkins runs on top of a Java web application
container or application server.
By default this is Jetty, but Jenkin's can be deployed
to any web application server with the right set up.
By default the Jenkins installer provides a packaged
version of Jetty in which Jenkins will run.
Since Jenkins runs on a Java web application
server, a java runtime is required.
You should run Jetty and Jenkins on a Java development
kit or JDK instead of just the Java runtime engine,
so Jenkins can access the JDK tools when needed.
On most operating systems there is
a small program called a service
that is responsible for starting stopping and
monitoring Jetty and the Jenkins application
Within Jenkins views provide a nice way
to group projects or folders together.
Projects also known as jobs provide configuration
or instructions on how to build software.
When ready projects will queue up until there is an available
build executer ready to execute the project's build.
Those executers running the Jenkins project
will leverage various supporting tools on the
host system to assist with the build process.
For example a Jenkins project may call a shell script.
a Maven lifecycle phase, or both.
Within Jenkins nearly everything is a plugin,
in that nearly all functionality is provided by plugins.
You can think of Jenkins as little
more than an executer of plugins.
Plugins are small libraries that add new abilities Jenkins
and can provide integration points for other tools
As we will see in the installation
section, Jenkins provides a set up wizard
that will recommend a curated collection of initial plugins
to get you started, which we will explore later on.
Of course, more plugins can be added later.
However some plugins can contain bugs, become abandoned, or
conflict with other plugins, or a myriad of other issues.
I recommend testing out any plugins before
introducing them to a live production system.
You might notice that projects are
sometimes referred to as jobs.
Jenkins appears to use these terms interchangeably.
As discussed before, projects are
the main unit of work in Jenkins,
by containing a list of instructions needed
to complete a build for a software project.
Those instructions are part of the Jenkins project's
configuration along with other project information,
like source control location and project metadata.
The project configuration is broken up into
general information, pre and post build step,
the main build steps, build phases, or other
build events potentially added by plugins.
Views are a way to group related projects together, and
really only serve to improve the user's interface in Jenkins.
There are global views that are available to everyone, although
they will only show the projects the user has access to.
Also, user defined views are set up in
private to the logged in user on Jenkins.
The default all view is provided
once the first project is created
and will contain all projects from that point forward.
The workspace is a location on the computer
system where Jenkins places all the files
related to the Jenkins project and related software project.
By default each project or job is
assigned a workspace location.
In addition to the software project
source code the workspace can contain
intermediate temporary log or build
files or Jenkins build metadata.
Upon success the workspace may also contain build artifacts.

by going to the start menu and
then select "computer management".
Once computer management system loads expand services
and applications and then click on services.
This will list all the services
currently set up on your system.
Find the item named Jenkins, then click on it to select it.
From here you can start, stop, or restart Jenkins.
Also you can configure Jenkins to start
automatically or not upon system reboot.
It is mainly important to know where the Jenkins service
is located just in case you really need to use it.

To confirm Java is correctly installed open your terminal

To start the installation process for Apache Maven open

"MAVEN_HOME", in all caps; "=/Development./Maven".
Then, on the next line, "export PATH"; space; and then,
in double quotes, we're going to set up our path.
First part of the path will have '$';
and then, in curly braces, "PATH".
That resolves to our current path variable.
Then we'll separate that with a colon; and
then add in our "MAVEN_HOME" variable; "/bin".
Now save and close the ".bashrc"
file and then restart terminal.
With the terminal restarted let's
verify Maven is set up correctly.

"localhost:8080" in the future to access Jenkins.

to walk through a simple getting started wizard.

the administrator password field on the screen.

Then install to continue.

giving us a completely clean install of Jenkins.

We will use this account most of the time initially
until we get to the security section of the course.
I'm going to call the username admin and
then provide a password and then confirm it.
I'm using "Admin Account" for the full name.
Once done click save and finish, and then start using Jenkins.
Now you should be on the main page of Jenkins.
Once installed Jenkins will be running as a service or daemon,
which means Jenkins will automatically be started
in the background whenever your system restarts.
If you need to you can use Mac's "Launch Daemon"
control tool to load and unload the Jenkins service.
That is "org.jenkins-ci.plist" via the command line.
Remember to issue those commands using
"sudo" since they require admin rights.

which usually follows the pattern. "apache-Maven-",

everyone can see into the Maven installation directory:
"chmod 755"; space; and then the directory name,
"apache-maven-" whatever version you have.

a shorter path for the install of Maven.

out different versions of maven later on:

currently have "apache-maven-" our version number;
space; and then simply "maven"; then press enter.
Now let's clean up after ourselves by deleting the downloaded
tarball: "rm"; space; and then the tarball filename.

make sure that we're in our user's home directory.

symbolic link, "/usr/local/maven".

the next path element, separated by colon, will reference
the "MAVEN_HOME" variable we just defined earlier,
"$"; then after that "/bin".

increase the number of build executers is available.

point for the rest of this course

Welcome, in this video, we're going to begin

Otherwise, if you reach this page
immediately after the installation,
then Jenkins may remember you from
before and place you on the dashboard.
Once on the dashboard notice, on the left hand side,
that we have several items which are high level;
we have our build queue, which there's nothing in
the queue, and we have our build executer status.
We'll go to this more as we build jobs.
And, right in the middle, when you first
start and there aren't any jobs created
you'll have a nice little inviting message to create a new job.
Let's do that now; click on the “Create new job” link.
We land on the new item or new job
page, which we will visit often.
In Jenkins jobs and projects are interchangeable,
so keep that in mind as we navigate around.
Since we did not install any plugins
during our installation process
we are presented with only one type of project that
comes natively with Jenkins: the Freestyle project.
Alright. Let's provide a name in
the item name field at the top.
Let's call our first job hello world and then click on the
freestyle project, which is the only option right now.
And finally click on the OK button.
The next screen is to actually configure this job.
Technically the job or project is already created and
available in Jenkins, but only with default configuration.
However most of the time we will want
to change the default configuration
by adding build steps or other instructions to the job
Before moving on let's review this page.
Again, since we didn't install any plugins,
there isn't a whole lot on this page yet.
However we can see that Jenkins can provide some
reasonable basic functionality as it stands now.
You can navigate around quickly by
clicking on the tabs at the top
to jump directly to specific parts of the configuration page.
Another reason why there's not a lot here is that
the bulk of the work can be done with build steps,
which are added individually, as well as post
build actions, which happen after the build.
Lets start off with our first build step.
I'm going to click on "Add build step",
which takes me to a nice drop down.
Since I'm on a Windows system I'm going to
select "Execute Windows batch command".
If you are on Mac or Linux choose execute shell instead.
Before entering the command in our command box here in Jenkins
let's try it out on the command line first.
In this case, since I'm running a Windows batch command,
I will need to open the windows command prompt,
which I have set up on my taskbar.
You can also find it on the start menu, which
you will find it in the Windows system folder.
If you are running Mac or Linux open your terminal.
In this particular case the command will work
exactly the same way in either shell environment.
The command we're going to use is "echo"
Type echo, space, and then double
quotes "hi", then press enter.
And, as you might imagine, the echo command simply echoes
or repeats whatever you pass into the echo command.
Such a simple command is practically universal.
This is all we're going to have Jenkins do right now.
Of course we're going to do much more elaborate things later.
Now that we're done with the command prompt or terminal
let's close it and return to Jenkins in the browser.
Back on the configuration page, in the build section,
type in the same command in the command text box:
"echo"; space; "hi" in double quotes.
Once we've done that we can click on the save button.
Great. After saving the configuration for our
first job it lands us on our project's main page.
I'm currently on the main page of
our project, also known as a job.
Since we're here I'm going to click on the build now
button to fire off our jobs execution. Click that now.
You can see the build was scheduled and responds very
quickly under the build history with a blue ball.
The blue ball actually means success,
and if we click on the ball we can
see the console output for that job.
Like I demonstrated a few moments ago using the "echo" command
simply prints out whatever you pass into it back to the
console, which Jenkins dutifully captures in its logs.
If I wanted to I could simply see the output as plain text.
But I will warn you that if you do that you
will need to use your browser's back button
to get back to the Jenkin's interface.
Another piece of the Jenkins UI is that
we need to take notice of the breadcrumb,
which is this listing where you are in the Jenkins UI.
We have Jenkins at the top level, hello world
which is the name of our job or project,
and then which build number I'm on, which I'm on build "#1".
I can click on build number one to return
to the main page for this specific build.

which I may do later but not right now.
I can click on "Back to project" or click
on "Hello World", which is our project.

--- Jenkins Dashboard ---

Welcome, in this video, we're going
to continue our Jenkins orientation
by looking at the additional features of the Jenkins interface.
I'm currently on our first project's main page.
In the build history we have a successful build.
If I go up another level, up to our main dashboard,
we now have our job listed under an all tab or view,
which will list, by default, all the jobs
we created in our Jenkins interface.
On this listing of our jobs we start off
with the first item, which is the status.
The status tells us the last known state of our build.
If you hover over your mouse over the icon you'll
get a short description of the image or icon.
Blue balls, by default, are success.
We'll change that later in another lesson.
The next column over is the weather,
which shows us an aggregated trend of all
the builds that have happened recently.
Sunshine means that all the recent
builds have successfully run.
Just like before hovering the mouse over it gives
us a description of the current weather icon.
Next we have the name of the job and how
long ago the last successful build was,
along with the build number of that successful build.
The next column over is last failure.
Since we don't have any failures "N/A" is displayed.
In the last column we have the last duration,
which is how long the last build took,
which is super-fast since we really didn't do very much.
And then, in the final column, we have a build now button,
which will allow us to schedule an immediate build.
By clicking on that I scheduled a build immediately,
which will happen so fast that we won't
even see it in the build executers.
We'll have to drop back down to
"Hello World", which is our project.
Once we arrive we will see that we have a
second build, which is also successful.
Alright. Let's return to the dashboard.
Observe the times have changed slightly.
Now the last success was updated to build number two.
At the bottom of the view table we can change the
size of the icons from small, medium, and large.
For now let's leave it on large.
Finally we have a few links that
are right under the table of jobs.
The first one is a link to a legend, which takes us to a page
describing all the different built status and weather icons.
We will see most of these actions throughout the course,
but this page is handy when first starting out.
After reviewing this page return to the dashboard.
The last three links are just different RSS feeds.
RSS is a variation of the ".xml" file format, which is a
specialized text format much like HTML for web pages.
RSS stands for Really Simple Syndication
and is a format that describes information
in a digestible, news feed like, data stream
for consumption and display by newsreaders.
Honestly RSS was a much bigger deal a few years
ago, but it is still important to some people.
If you click on one of the RSS links
you'll need to use your browser's back button or
functionality to return back to the Jenkins dashboard.
In this lesson we took a brief look at the Jenkins
dashboard now that we have a job for it to display.
Next we will fail the build on purpose
and then disable and re-enable jobs.
Of course we will return to the dashboard to see
how each of these job states impact the view.

=== Bonus: Full Install on Windows ===


--- Copy and Rename Jobs ---

Welcome, in this lesson, we are going to copy and
rename our job to create more jobs for us to work with.
Sometimes we make mistakes or we just
change our minds about how jobs are named.
That's ok, since it's rather easy to
rename a job or project in Jenkins.
From the dashboard, go to your job.
Once on the job status page notice how the
name of the project is part of the URL.
You can grab this URL from your web browser's
address bar and save it for later,
bookmark it or email it to a coworker: whatever you might
want to do when you want to share a specific page you're on.
In this case the URL also has embedded spaces as encoded "%20".
I'm not a fan of embedded spaces in URLs.
We can fix that, but first let's rename the project entirely.
Go to the configure page for this project.
At the top the name can be changed.
Doing so renames the project and it will affect the
URL associated with the project and any sub-pages.
When we change the name to "Problem World", then click save.
When we do we will be presented with a confirmation
screen since this is potentially a disruptive change.
Click yes to continue.
Notice how the URL changed, again still with spaces.
Let's change the name again by going back into configure.
We can actually keep the friendly name
while also making the web page URL better.
However the way that Jenkins saves the
rename we will have to do this in two steps.
Before we change the name copy the current
name into your system's clipboard.
Let's rename the project again to a shorter
name that will be used for the URL.
The "Project name" field controls the URL used by
Jenkins to refer to the project and related pages.
Now let's pick a name that is more web friendly.
I prefer names that are short, all lowercase, and
do not contain spaces or special characters.
Let's use "problem" as the name.
Great, now save the change.
Again proceed through the confirmation page.
Great. Now the project URL is a lot better
and web friendly, but not as user friendly.
Now return to the configure page.
Once back there let's do two things.
First let's provide a description;
it really doesn't matter what you type,
I'm going to use "A problematic project".
Then, instead of clicking save, click Apply.
That will save the project's configuration
without leaving the configure page.
You should see a status banner briefly
appear at the top saying "Saved".
Now, while still in the general section,
find the “Advanced” button and click on it.
It will expand out several more options.
Scroll down a bit and find the item "Display Name".
Within that field paste the value that is hopefully still
in your clipboard, which should be "Problem World".
Now click on Save,
which will return us to our project's main or
status page after saving its new configuration.
Now, look at the changes.
The web URL remains the same short path from earlier
but the display name is used for most other
places, including on the bread crumb trail.
This will be the name displayed to the user most of the time,
which we can see on this main project page
along with the description we entered.

characters before problem is displayed below.

--- Update Plugins ---

Welcome, in this lesson, we are going to research,
install, and update plugins in Jenkins.
As I mentioned before plugins are the main way
to extend functionality provided by Jenkins.
Jenkins does actually come with some native
functionality, like running batch or shell scripts,
but the native features are fairly limited.
Most people install many plugins to
provide a rich feature set for Jenkins.
In fact the set up wizard during the installation process
recommended several very common and specially
vetted plugins to start things off.
However I walked you through installing Jenkins
without any initial plug ins to keep things simple.
Now let's install a few plug ins directly inside of Jenkins.
From the dashboard cick on the "Manage Jenkins" menu link.
This is the manage Jenkins page, which will go over more later.
For now click on the manage plugins item,
which brings us to the plugin manager page.
Once there you will land on the update tab.
This page would list all the plugins that need updating;
however, since we don't have any
plugins, nothing needs updating.
If we jump over to installed we can
confirm no plugins are installed.
We need several plugins to start out and
there are several ways to install plugins.
The easiest, and the way I recommend
the most, is to use the available tab.
The problem is this tab can be a
little cumbersome to deal with.
The best thing to do is to research on the Jenkins web site
and then use the available have to
select the plug ins to be installed.
Open another window or tab in your browser to "jenkins.io".
Find the "Plugins" menu item.
Keep in mind that I don't have any control over
this website, so it might change a bit over time.
Once on the main plugins page do a search for the
first plug in we need to install: the Git plugin.
When we search we will see it among the top
results, perhaps even the first result provided.
Click on that item so we can confirm that is what we want.
Once on the Git plugin page review this
page about this particular plugin.
You'll notice things, like the last
release date, which is hopefully recent
and a list of dependencies, which
Jenkins may install automatically.
As we scroll down we will see other information,
like important warnings or notes about this plugin,
perhaps documentation on how to use it
and even a change log for the plug in.
Not all plug ins had this level of support and documentation.
Anyway scroll back to the top and you'll notice the name.
Back in Jenkins, while on the available tab, use
the filter search box and type in "git plugin".
The plug in we want should be listed.
You can confirm this by right clicking and opening the
link in a tab from the listing you're interested.
As we were researching before, you
certainly have the right plug in.
Now, back in Jenkins, check the box next to the "Git
plugin", and then click on "Install without restart".
That option allows Jenkins to download and
install the plugin without requiring a restart.
This isn't always a good option, but it's worth trying.
If the plugin ends up installing a bunch of dependencies
it's best to restart Jenkins, just to be safe.
Great, we got several plugins
installed simply by selecting one.
The Jenkins plugin install process will automatically
resolve download and install any plugin dependencies
Before we restart Jenkins I want to install a plugin manually.
Return to the web browser tab on the Jenkins
plugins page, and then return to the search page.
Then search for "greenballs".
This plugin will change all blue balls to green.
Once on the green balls plugin page, while you
can review this page like any other plugin,
what I'm interested in is the archives.
Normally I'm happy with the latest version of plugins,
but in some cases you might want to
install an older version directly.
Click on "Archives", and then click on any past version.
I'm going with 1.14.
Click on that item, which downloads an ".hpi" file,
which is basically like a zip file that
Jenkins is expecting to contain a plug in.
Once the ".hpi" file finishes downloading return to
Jenkins and navigate back to the plugin manager page.
Now click on the "Advanced" tab.
Now scroll down until you find the "Upload Plugin" item.
In the file field choose the file on your local
system, probably in the "Downloads" folder.
And then click "Upload", right underneath.
With all these plugins installed it's best to restart Jenkins.
You can do that with your operating system's service commands.
However, for a simple restart, that
can be done directly in the browser.
Return to the Jenkins dashboard and, once there,
modify your web browser's address bar to add
"/restart" to the URL, then press enter.
If you did that Jenkins should send you to a confirmation page.
Click on the "Yes" button to continue.
Once Jenkins is back you'll probably
end up on the login screen.
If so you can check the "Remember me" check box.
Now log in like normal.
Once back on the Jenkins dashboard
the blue icon has turned green.
As you can imagine that's the work of the green balls plugin.
Let's return to the plugin manager one more time.
This time I can use the bread crumb
trail menus to go directly there.
Once back on the plugin manager we now see the
green balls plugin has an update available.
Of course I just walked through how
to install an older release manually
so naturally that particular plugin
has a newer version available.
You and your team should determine a
good update policy and testing strategy.
Some plugins or updates can cause conflicts
or stop working with your existing projects.
I recommend reviewing each plugin's release
notes that you are considering updating
and testing everything on a non-production instance of Jenkins.
However, in this case, I know that the
latest version of green balls is safe.
You will also notice that we have a last
update notice along with a "Check Now" button.
This is useful to get the latest update
information before applying updates.
After the update check the box next to green balls,
and then click on "Download now and install
after restart", which is the safest approach.
Once the download is completed, along
with any possible dependencies,
we can check the box next to the restart option.
Or you can use the URL trick like before.
If Jenkins doesn't refresh within a few minutes,
or if the web page comes back a bit strange,
just refresh the page in your browser or return to
the Jenkins dashboard using a link on the page.
Once back on the dashboard let's
return to the plugin manager again.
This time go to the installed tab.
We have a fairly long list of plugins
that are installed in Jenkins right now.
The grayed out plugins are the ones
that are dependencies of other plugins,
which means they can't be uninstalled or disabled
without first doing the same to the top level plug in.
If you click on one Jenkins will tell you that it can't be
disabled or uninstalled, and which plugins depend on it.
Some dependency plugins have
multiple plugins that depend on it.
For example the "Mailer Plugin" has several plugins like that.
Lets disable the green balls plugin
by unchecking the box next to it.
Then scroll down to the very bottom of the page.
There should be a button to allow you to restart.
Click that button.
If that is not visible for some reason, make
sure you unchecked the green balls plug in
and use the restart URL trick to force a restart of Jenkins.
Once back on the Jenkins dashboard the green balls plugin
will be disabled and the green status will be back to blue.
Sometimes you need to disable or even uninstall plugins that
are acting up or otherwise not playing nice with others.
Well, I actually liked the green balls plug
in, so I will return to the plugin manager
and go back to the "Installed" tab, and
check the box to enable green balls.
Now restart Jenkins and return to the dashboard
Green balls should be back.
In this lesson we covered researching
and managing plugins in Jenkins.

when I'm just trying to debug the build process.

unless I had a very compelling reason.

the first five builds are very sacred.

--- Manage Jenkins ---

Welcome, in this lesson, we are going to briefly look
at some items within the management section of Jenkins.
Form the Jenkins dashboard click on the
"Manage Jenkins" menu item on the left,
which takes us to the manage Jenkins main page.
That menu item, and the ability to
access the management pages of Jenkins,
depends on the access level of your logged in user.
Starting in Jenkins 2 the installation process
requires setting up an admin account,
which is the account we are logged in as right now.
We will cover security a bit later, but right
now we are logged in as an admin user,
which allows us to access all the management pages on Jenkins.
While we are here I'll briefly cover several of the items
before moving on to the system and tool configuration.
The first item, which we will cover a bit later, is
the main configuration page for the Jenkins server.
Global security, also something we
will cover later on in this course,
is where we will configure how people log in
and general security policies for the server.
Credentials is where we set up and manage passwords, API keys,
SSH keys and other credentials we would use with
plugins or connecting to other systems from Jenkins.
Global tools, which we will cover soon,
is where we will configure the tools we will
use with Jenkins, like Git, Java, and Maven
"Reload Configuration from Disk" does exactly what you think:
it reloads the Jenkins configuration from the system disk.
We'll cover this a bit later, but Jenkins' configuration is
stored in files in its installation directory on the system.
You would use this link if you made manual changes
to those files directly on the file system.
If you click it you will be prompted if you
really want to do this. I'll cancel for now.
Manage plugins is something we just covered.
System information contains system properties, which
a lot comes directly from the Java runtime engine.
We also have a list of environment variables Jenkins can use
and a list of plugins that are installed, their
version numbers, and if they are enabled or not.
Finally a link to the thread dumps page, which
is useful for very advanced troubleshooting.
Click on "Manage Jenkins" to return
to the main management page.
System log provides access to the log messages for Jenkins.
Load statistics is really more interesting
when we have a lot of activity on the server.
But, for now, there really isn't anything to see.
The Jenkins CLI can provide instructions and
access to the Jenkins command line tool,
which is really for advanced use cases like
automation and scripting tasks in Jenkins.
Script console is another advanced tool.
You can run Groovy code to interact
with Jenkins via an internal API.
If we run this provided example, we will get a list of plugins.
Of course you could do a bunch of cool things here but it
requires experience with the Groovy programming language.
Manage nodes is where we manage the build nodes, which is
where Jenkins actually runs builds including the master.
Jenkins supports scaling by adding build nodes
and, depending on the plugins installed, there
are many types of build nodes that can be used.
About Jenkins, this is what it sounds
like an about page for Jenkins.
It is neat to see how the Jenkins server is built
upon many open source components and libraries
in addition to the Jenkins source code itself.
We also have the links to each of the
licenses' pages for each of the plugins used.
Manage old data is only relevant if you update
to a newer version of Jenkins or plugins
and the configuration loaded on the file system
is designed to work for an older version
of Jenkins or the updated plugins.
Manage users is where we manage users while using
Jenkins' internal access management database,
which we are currently using.
We'll talk about this more in the
security section of the course.
Finally, prepare for shutdown.
This doesn't actually shut down the Jenkins server;
instead it helps you manage the quieting process.
That is, it puts up a banner informing users of an
upcoming shutdown and prevents new builds from starting.
Once all in progress builds complete you
can then turn off or restart Jenkins
using the Jenkins service or the restart URL trick.
That's all we have for now.
Of course, if you install plugins that provide
some type of configuration or management feature
this page will change to include that here as well.
We briefly looked at each of the items
on the main Jenkins management page.
While there are some items here that are
simply out of scope for this course
we have or will cover most of them, at least a little bit.
In fact our next lesson will go into the configure system page.

--- Jenkins Configuration ---

Welcome, in this video, we're going to continue our Jenkins
orientation by setting up our Jenkins configuration.
We should be already the main manage Jenkins page.
If not navigate there now.
From manage Jenkins click on the top item, "Configure System".
Right now there isn't a lot here; this page will expand
greatly as we add more plugins to our Jenkins server
and we will be making occasional
trips back to this page accordingly.
We can take a quick look around before changing any values.
If you want to know about something you can use the
question mark icon to expand out some help text.
For now there are just two settings we will look at.
First we have the number of executers.
That refers to the number of
concurrent build threads or processes
that we can have at the same time, specifically on the master.
By default Jenkins sets this value to 2,
which, for most systems, is rather low.
I recommend setting it to a value somewhere around
1 to 2 times the number of CPU or cores
you have available on your system.
For example, if you are running on 4
cores consider a setting between 4 to 8.
What about hyper-threading or similar technology?
Well I still recommend limiting it
to two to three times per core.
In a four core system with hyper threading you could
probably use 8 to 12 executers without too many issues.
You will likely need to observe the performance
and make adjustments in order to find
the right setting for your system.
I'm going to stay relatively conservative with 4 executers.
Once there are four concurrent builds going on
any additional builds will queue up
until an executer becomes available.
After I set the value to 4 click apply so that we
can save the configuration and remain on this page
To see the results of the setting
change just refresh your browser page.
Now the build executers is just expanded to four.
The other setting to review is the system message.
This places a message at the top of the Jenkins dashboard,
which is great when making announcements to your users,
like a planned maintenance window on the weekend.
Let's try it out: "Planned downtime starting tomorrow at
11 pm and will last for 30 minutes for system updates".
Once you have typed in that message click
"Save" to save the configuration changes
and return to the Jenkins dashboard.
There we will see a system message displayed.
Now return to the system page in "Manage Jenkins".
Once there remove the system message and click save.
Now the message is gone.
Great. Let's return to "Manage Jenkins".
Finally it is time to configure the
tools we are going to use with Jenkins.
Click on "Global Tool Configuration".
Here we will set up or configure Jenkins, Java, and Maven.
Let's start with the Java JDK.
So under JDK, JDK installations, click on "Add JDK".
By default Jenkins is going to have
"Install automatically" checked,
but we already installed the JDK so I'm
going to uncheck install automatically
Instead we need to fill in the name, which is any
label we want to give this installation of the JDK,
as well as the Java home, which is the full
path to where Java is currently installed.
Since we installed Java and the JDK earlier
you should know where that is located.
For this I can use a trick;
since I know the Maven's version command will
give us both the path to the JDK and Maven home
I can use one command to get both pieces of information.
I'll open up Git bash on Windows or my terminal on Mac
OS or Linux and then type "mvn -v", then press enter.
The Java home should be displayed,
probably to the "jre" beneath the JDK.
So only copy the path to the JDK, then return to
Jenkins and paste the value into the Java home field.
Since this is a Java 8 or JDK 1.8 installation I
will give the name "Java 8" in the name field,
then click Apply to save the changes so far.
Next we have Git.
By default Git will be configured to use the Git command.
As long as Jenkins has access to the Git command,
you won't need to specify the full path.
If, for some reason, Jenkins isn't able to reach the
Git command you will need to find Git on your system
and update the path to the Git executable
field to the full path to the Git command.
If you install Git, like we covered
earlier, you should be fine.
Let's continue on with the Maven installation settings.
We already have easy access to that information;
we're going to be following the same process.
Click on "Add Maven".
Let's give this Maven installation a name.
I'm going to uncheck install automatically and, like before,
Maven home is the full path to where we have Maven installed
Return to Git bash or your terminal and copy
the Maven home value to your clipboard.
We're actually done with Git bash or terminal, so exit out.
Then return to Jenkins and paste the
Maven path into the Maven home field.
Since there are no further changes to
make I'm going to click on "Save".
Great. Now we have returned to manage Jenkins.
Since we are done with system configuration and
management pages return to the Jenkins dashboard.
We just made several changes to our Jenkins system,
but we really won't see the benefits of
those changes until the next section,
where we will set up Maven specific projects in Jenkins.

--- Confirm Maven Project Outside Jenkins ---

Welcome, in this video, we're going to
download and compile the next application
we're going to be using in this
section outside of Jenkins first.

using for the rest of this section

project called time-tracker. Click on that link.
This is a project that we will be using for the rest of
this section to demo the Maven based workflows in Jenkins.
However, I don't want you to work
directly with this repository;
Instead we will need to fork this repository
into your own GitHub account space.
That will allow us to make changes to the repository
and see the results accordingly in Jenkins.
To fork this repository, click on the fork button in the
top right-hand corner of the repository's main page.
Depending on whether or not you're
part of other GitHub organizations
you might see an intermediate page to
select where to fork this repository into.
For most other users you'll proceed directly to your
newly forked repository located on your account.
Once you have forked a copy of the time tracker Maven project
take a few moments to review the source
code that makes up this repository.
The top level has a few files and two folders.
The files are ".gitignore", which is a special file used
by Git to exclude unwanted files from the repository,
which is mostly useful for the developers of the project.
A license file that explains the license
agreement governing the source code.
On the public GitHub repositories that will normally
be some type of open source compatible license.
The ReadMe file, which is in markdown format,
which is a common way developers provide documentation
about their project and the repository.
And finally the maven ".pom" file, which describes
this project's dependency list and build instructions.
The ".pom" file also defines the submodules.
If we go into one of the folders each one
represents a Maven submodule project,
which, on its own, is a full Maven project.
from here the directory and file structure
is that of a normal Maven project.
I'll leave it at that; If you need more
information on how Maven projects work
you could check out my other courses that focus on Maven.
However, I'll walk you through everything you need to know
regarding how to build Maven projects
using Jenkins in this course.
Now that we have our own copy of the repository
let's make sure this project compiles outside of
Jenkins before we get started inside of Jenkins.
To do that, we need to clone this
repository to our local system.
To access the clone URL for this repository
click on the green "Clone or download"
button, which expands out some options.
Unless you know what you're doing
make sure you choose "Use HTTPS".
That will give us the HTTPS clone URL.
You can either copy the URL in the text
box or use the copy to clipboard button.
Either way you should have the HTTPS
URL in your system's clipboard.
On Windows we need to verify our steps
using the Windows command prompt
since that is the environment in which Jenkins
will run our Maven commands later on.
For Mac and Linux use your terminal program,
I'll point out the commands that are different.
Type "git clone"; space; then paste in the GitHub URL
that should be in your clipboard; then press enter.
Once the clone command has completed use the cd command
to change directory into the "time-tracker" folder.
"cd time-tracker", then press enter.
Then I'll use the "dir" command on Windows,
or the "ls" command or Mac or Linux
to list the files and folders at
the main level of the repository.
Since this project has a "pom.xml" this should be able
to run the Maven command directly from this folder.
Type in "mvn clean package", then press enter.
Once the Maven command has completed make sure
that we have a build success towards the end.
If you do that means the project is
in a successfully building state.
Now that we've confirmed this repository
and its containing projects will build,
we will close the Windows command
prompt or your terminal program.
I think it is important to remove Jenkins from the
troubleshooting process by attempting to run the Maven project,
or really any project, in the command line
environment that will be used by Jenkins.
Now that we have confirmed the Maven
project is working outside of Jenkins
we can now create a job to build the Maven

Notice we aren't using any directory commands;
Jenkins will run the command specified
in the workspace for this project.
The workspace is the location where
Jenkins performs the active build,
and that directory is kept until the next build occurs,
which means we can inspect it after the build completes.
Jenkins will check out the code from the specified
Git repository, which is hosted on GitHub,
and place all the files and folders
in the workspace during the build.
After that Jenkins will execute any commands in the
command box in that workspace folder location.
When Maven runs it will look for its configuration
file named "pom.xml" in the current directory.
Now that we know how this job will work click "Save".
Once on the project status page, which is the default page
for a project, click on "Build Now" to start a build.
You should notice that it takes much
longer than the previous examples.
Of course Maven is doing much more, including
downloading a bunch of dependencies
before compiling and packaging up our built artifact.
Once the build is done refresh the browser page
to see the most up to date version of this page.
Hopefully this build was successful.
Click on the date of the build to access the main build page.
Once there you'll notice a few changes;
First, since this is a Git based project,
we have a few items related to that,
like the revision information in the middle of the
screen and a Git build data page for this build.
That page provides some Git specific information,
like the commit or branch used during the build.
Now, if we click on the console output,
we should see a lot more stuff here.
In fact you might even see a link
to the "Full Log" near the top
if any part of the build log was
omitted because of excessive length.
Clicking on that refreshes this page
with the full log, which is quite long.
However we can see the start of the log, including who
started it and where the workspace is located on our system.
We will also see the beginning steps of our build, which starts
with a bunch of Git commands provided by the Git plug in.
Then we will see the Maven command entered
in the command box for our build step.
The rest of the log is the output from the
Maven command, which is also captured here
and should look similar to the output of
the Maven command in the previous lesson.
If I scroll all the way to the end,
we should see the final parts of the Maven command
which ends with "BUILD SUCCESS" in all caps.
The last line comes from Jenkins.
Since Jenkins detects the batch or shell command executed
successfully Jenkins marks this build as successful.
Alright, let's return to the main page of our project.
This is one way to build a Maven project;
simply specify the Maven commands exactly as
you would in the command prompt or terminal.
This approach works just fine and can be applied to just about
any technology or build tools that run off the command line.
However Jenkins provides a Maven specific build step.
Now return to the configure page for this
project, then jump down to the build section
Remove the current build step and replace it with a
new build step, "Invoke top level Maven targets".
When you choose that option you will get a different set up.
We can choose which Maven install, which,
in this case, we can either specify Maven 3
or leave it as default; I'm going to choose Maven 3.
Then we can specify the Maven goals to run.
Jenkins will assume the "mvn" command, so
only specify the goals "clean package".
If you click on the "Advanced" button we are
given a lot more settings we can adjust.
If you have any questions about any of them
just click on the blue question mark to get
more information about a particular setting.
I'm not going to change anything right now, so just
click on "Save" to return to the project page.
Once there click on "Build Now" to trigger another build.
This time the build will go a bit faster since Maven has
already downloaded all the dependencies needed for the project
So let's check out the console output for the new build.
This time Jenkins didn't need to provide the full log
option since the logs for this build were much shorter.
We have the standard stuff at the top,
but, right after the "git" command,
we have the command Jenkins used to build our Maven project.
It's actually a bit different than the command
we used earlier, but it gets the job done.
In fact, if we look around the other
build pages, there isn't any differences
between using a batch or a shell command
build step and a Maven specific build step;
they both end up doing the same thing in the end.
However, since the Maven build step provides
easy access to more advanced Maven options,
and that build step can be used regardless
of the underlying operating system,
I tend to lean towards that build step when building
Maven projects with the freestyle project in Jenkins.
For now let's return to the Jenkins dashboard.
Once there we will see our project listed
in the all tab, like we've seen before.
In this lesson we set up a Maven base Java project to
build using the freestyle project or job in Jenkins.
We looked at using both a command build
step and then a Maven specific build step.

contents of the project's workspace.

--- Maven Jobs ---

Welcome, in this lesson, we are going to install a new plugin
that will provide greater integration
between Jenkins and Maven.
And then we will create and build
a new project to use that plugin.
In an earlier lesson we created a project, Maven freestyle,
that simply use the existing freestyle project in Jenkins
to build a Java based project using Apache Maven.
While that approach works very well
I like to use a specific plugin,
which once was provided by Jenkins by
default, called the Maven integration plugin.
This plugin provides a great integration
between Maven projects and Jenkins.
I will warn you that not everyone believes
using this plug in is a good idea.
There are several reasons why.
First it's yet another plug in to install in Jenkins;
the more plugins you install and use the
slower your installation of Jenkins becomes.
That's one reason why I started Jenkins with no plugins
and only added the plugins we needed at the time.
Second, because of the deeper Jenkins integration with
Maven, builds that use this plugin will be slower
than simply using the freestyle project, which
gives up that integration for better performance.
Third the Maven integration plugin has a
lot of other plugins as dependencies.
To balance things out I've provided
some counter arguments to those points.
First, many plugins actually depend
on the Maven integration plugin,
especially since this plugin used to be bundled with Jenkins.
Either way you'll probably eventually
have this plugin installed.
Second, while it is true builds with the
Maven integration plugin do run a bit longer
I think it is worth it for the level of
integration you get while using the plug in.
Ultimately you should weigh your options,
perhaps even perform some tests with both
approaches to see which one is a better fit.
Let's assume you're OK giving up a bit of performance for
some really nice Maven goodness directly in Jenkins.
Starting in Jenkins 2 you now have to install
the Maven integration plug in separately.
Oddly enough it's not even featured among the
recommended set of plugins during the set up wizard,
which I find strange considering it
used to be installed by default.
Like we covered in the plugin lesson go to the
plug in manager page under Manage Jenkins.
Once there click on the available tab.
Since I'm telling you which plug in to
install we can skip the research part.
Simply type "Maven" in the filter search field.
Once the results for the filter search returns the Maven
integration plugin should be among the top results.
Select that plugin with a check next to that plugin.
While we're here, if the config file plugin
is listed, check that plugin as well.
Now click on the "Download now and install after
restart" button, since that is the safest option.
While the plugins, including any dependency, downloads
check the box to restart Jenkins once done.
If, for some reason, Jenkins doesn't update after a while
try refreshing your browser page or return to the
Jenkins dashboard and do a restart from there
using the URL trick demonstrated earlier.
Once Jenkins has completed restarting, from
the Jenkins dashboard, click on new item.
For the project name use "Time Tracker", and then choose
the newly added Maven project, and finally click OK.
Great, let's configure our Maven project.
Since this is a different Jenkins project type we
should review this page and observe any differences.
For example, there are a few more sections
and if we scroll down through the page
some of the previous sections have additional
settings that are specific to Maven builds
or perhaps introduced by dependency
plugins installed along the way.
OK, jump to source control management, select Git.
On another web browser tab I have
my time tracker GitHub repository.
Like we've done earlier, grab the
HTTPS clone URL for this repository.
Go back to Jenkins and paste in our repository URL.
Like in the previous examples, all
the other Git settings are fine.
Now jump down to the build section.
This section is very different;
before you could choose from a
dropdown for specific build steps.
Now we have two fields that are Maven
specific: "Root POM" and "Goals and options".
Instead the Maven build section is
surrounded by pre and post build steps.
Basically, if we needed to do other
build steps besides the Maven build
the Maven integration plugin provides a way to accomplish that.
Let's look at the two fields we have here:
"Root POM", this is the main "pom.xml" for this project.
By default it is simply "pom.xml", which resolves to the
root or main folder of the workspace for this build.
For goals and options we simply specify the top
level Maven goals or lifecycle phases to target,
along with any command line options needed by the Maven build.
We need to specify "clean package" here.
If you want you can click on the "Advanced"
button to expand out even more options.
We won't need to change anything here, but it is
interesting to look at all the available options.
Feel free to use the blue question mark to
show some help text on a particular setting.
At this point I'm done with any further
changes to this job's configuration.
So click on "Save", which will land
us on the project's status page.

--- Scheduling Jenkins Jobs ---

Welcome, in this video, we're going to cover
how to automate builds by scheduling them.
I'm currently on my Maven project's main page.
Let's configure our build, click on configure.
Once the configuration page loads scroll down
until you find the "Build Triggers" section.
There are all the various ways that
this build project can be triggered.
If you're not sure how something works, most of the time
Jenkins provides a question mark that you can expand out.
This explains what happens or what
the purpose of that setting is.
When you're done with the help you can just
click on the help icon again to collapse it.
The item we're going to look at is “build periodically”,
so check that item and that expands
out to a schedule and an open field.
Let's see how the schedule field works
by expanding out the question mark.
It tells us how the schedule field works, which
is much like a crontab on a Linux system.
That means we have five positions: the minute position,
the hour position, the day of the month position,
the month position, and the day of the week position.
By putting a value in each one of these positions
we can schedule when this job needs to run.
It tells us that each one of these positions
we can enter some type of numeric value.
For example, in the minute position, it's
0 through 59 to represent all 60 minutes.
The hour, it's 0 through 23 and so on.
There are also special values that we can add, which is an
asterisk for all valid values for a particular position.
We can also enter a range, the
first value until the last value.
We can even do a little bit of math, that
is a range then divided by another value,
or all the values divided by a particular value.
There's also a special "H" symbol which means
that you can run within a degree of flexibility,
that is somewhere between one value and another value.
We also have some aliases that provide a more
user friendly way of representing a job schedule.
For example "@yearly", "@annually", "@monthly",
"@weekly", "@daily", "@midnight", or "@hourly".
Alright, let's scroll up and give this a try.
Alright, I've entered a value, that is "*/3",
with the asterisks in the other four positions effectively
allowing this job to run every single third minute.
Also, as you move off of the schedule
field, there may pop up a little help text
that says that you might want to use
this syntax instead of that syntax.
So let's make the change it recommends:
instead of "*/3", I'm going to say "H/3".
Now, when I click away from the field,
this message gives me an indication
of when this job would have ran,
and when it's going to run next.
Let's go ahead and save the change, and wait
for the build to trigger automatically.
Great, our build has automatically been triggered.
In this lesson we configured our Maven
project to build upon a regular schedule.
Of course, this approach will build the project
regardless if anything changed in the source repository.
In the next lesson we will address that issue by
switching out the schedule build with polling.

--- Source Control Polling in Jenkins ---

In this video we're going to continue
looking at other project build triggers.
We're starting out on our Maven project's Build status page.
Head over to configure.
Scroll down until you find "Build Triggers".
Right now we have it set to build periodically.
This will trigger a build based on the schedule, regardless
if there are changes in the source control or not.
Instead I want to poll my source
control repository on this schedule
and only build if changes have been detected.
I'm going to select this schedule and right click and copy.
Now I'm going to uncheck "Build
periodically" and then check "Poll SCM".
This provides me the same schedule
field, with the same type of format.
Now paste in the value from your clipboard.
Once we save this project configuration the project
will poll or look at the source control system
on the schedule instead of automatically building.
If Jenkins detects a change in the source control,
based on the polling, then it will trigger a build.
Now let's head over to GitHub and make a
change to our project within our repository.
I've gone to the main repository page for the repository.
And let's just update the ReadMe file.
So click on "README.md", and then
click on the edit this file icon.
I just made a slight tweak to the ReadMe file,
adding a little purpose section and
then a single sentence afterwards.
Once I've made our change I'm going to
add a commit message: "Updating README",
and then click on commit changes.
Let's head back over to Jenkins and
wait for the next build to build.
Great, it looks like Jenkins detected our change.
In fact we end up with an extra item in our menu
Section, "Git Polling Log". Let's check it out.
This shows us what Jenkins did to check out the changes.
And, at the end, it says changes were
found, which of course triggered the build.
I like the idea of polling GitHub occasionally
to see if there are any new changes,
but I think every three minutes is probably
excessive now that we know this works.
So, let's return to our project's configure page.
Let's poll for updates every 15 minutes instead.
Change our value so now we have "H/15".
And, since I want to continue making changes but I
want to make sure the setting is actually saved,
I'm going to hit apply instead of save.
We get a message at the top saying it's saved, but we
remain on the configuration page to allow further edits.
Great, let's scroll up and make another quick
change; let's add a nice little description here:
"A simple time-tracking application".
Now, if we click on Save, we will
return to the project's main page,
and we see our description, "A simple time-tracking
application" listed below the title.
Great, let's return to our dashboard.
In this lesson we changed our project's configuration to
poll our source control repository on a regular basis,
to look for updates and only build if updates are detected.

--- Upgrading Jenkins ---

In this video we're going to take a few moments
to upgrade Jenkins to the latest LTS version,
and also make sure our plugins are up to date as well.
Some might not realize that creating a
polished course takes a lot of time.
Because of that software that updates frequently
can change during the recording of a course.
Jenkins is a good example of this; not only does the
core Jenkins software need to be updated periodically
but plugins often need to be updated within
just a few days of initial installation.
This provides me the opportunity to share
the upgrade or update process with you,
which is a bit different depending on your operating system.
If you go to the Jenkins dashboard and you are logged in as
the admin account you might notice a red box at the top.
This tells us there are one or more Jenkins
management issues that need our attention.
We can get a brief summary by hovering the mouse over
and, if we click on the box, we get
an even more detailed list of issues.
In this case we are informed of a new release
of Jenkins and plugin updates available.
In my specific case I have several warnings about
some of the outdated plugins currently installed.
In either case it's a good time to update.
Oftentimes both Jenkins and plugins need to be updated.
In that case I recommend updating Jenkins first, since
that might influence updates to plugins afterwards.
Go to "Manage Jenkins".
Once there we have the same warnings we saw
from the Jenkins dashboard warning box.
From here we can review the changelog in another browser tab.
I always review the Jenkins change log before upgrading,
especially on production or critical systems.
I've been bitten by bad updates more than once
or, more commonly, good updates that happen
to break my current project configurations.
Once you're done reviewing the change log return to Jenkins.
Since I'm currently on Windows I'll walk through
the Jenkins upgrade process here first.
On Windows the easiest way to upgrade Jenkins is to
do it automatically from the manage Jenkins page.
Click on the upgrade automatically button,
which takes us to the update Center,
which is normally used for updating plugins.
After a little while you should get a
success next to the "jenkins.war" item,
since that is how Jenkins is packaged.
Once the underlying war file for Jenkins has
completed downloading we need to restart Jenkins.
Pick your favorite way to restart Jenkins.
Once back on the Jenkins dashboard, make
sure you are logged in as the admin user.
You should notice the installed
version has been increased a bit.
Before moving on to updating the plugins let's look
at how to upgrade Jenkins on the other platforms.
For mac os the easiest way to upgrade Jenkins is to
simply install the latest Mac OS installer image,
which is available from "jenkins.io", on
top of the existing Jenkins installation.
the installer will restart Jenkins after the upgrade and it
won't require you to go through the getting started wizard.
Once the reinstallation is complete the installer will
launch the default browser to go to the Jenkins dashboard.
Once there you should notice Jenkins has been upgraded.
For Linux, and specifically Linux Mint,
we just need to run the package manager
to update or upgrade our existing installed packages.
Of course you'll need to do this as root or sudo the command.
Jenkins should be included in this list of available updates:
"sudo apt-get update" then "sudo apt-get upgrade -y".
Once Jenkins has been upgraded, along
with any other system packages,
restart Jenkins using the service
management tool, in this case "systemctl".
Then return to the Jenkins dashboard using your
browser to confirm Jenkins has been upgraded.
Now that Jenkins is up to date let's update our plugins.
Since we've already covered updating plugins
that are already installed on Jenkins,
we will breeze through this process again.

absolutely most up to date information.

we can confirm that the updates tab
in the plugin manager is empty.

since Maven normally runs with a much older default.
Then we have the three plugins we will use for
quality analysis: Findbugs, PMD, and Checkstyle.
The first two, Findbugs and PMD, are used to find known
coding errors, or at least not recommended coding practices.
Checkstyle, as the name implies, checks your
code against a recommended coding style
or conventions and reports any deviations to standard.
OK, that's all I need to cover in the main Pom.
Underneath core, if I go to the Pom XML,

file, which is like a zip file used by Java projects.

The point is that this module will produce a

which will be used by the web module as a dependency.
Underneath all that we have our dependencies defined.
That is a list of libraries needed to
compile and or run the code in this module.

structures that basically mirror each other,

the other contains unit testing under test.
That is each directory structure
contains the directory or folder
for the specific language or
technology used, in this case Java.
Both main and test contain a Java folder,
which is followed by a matching set of folders that
make up the package name used by Java projects.
Packages in Java are like unique

nested folders for each part of the package name.

--- Quality Analysis Plugins and Configuration ---

Welcome, in this video, we're going to install the
plugins we will need for the rest of this section
and configure our project to use those plugins.
Let's verify that the unit test
plug in for Jenkins is installed.
Click on Manage Jenkins, then go to
Manage Plugins and click on installed.
The JUnit plug in should already be installed.
If the JUnit plug in is not installed then you
need to install it from the available tab,
like we have done with other plugins.
While we are here let's install the other
plugins we'll need in this section.
The plugins we need to install are
for PMD, Findbugs, and Checkstyle.
Let's search for PMD first.
Find the PMD plug in and check it.
Now go back up to the filter and type and findbugs
find the Findbugs plug in and check it.
Go back up to the filter and type checkstyle,
and find the Checkstyle plugin, and check it.
Then click on "Download now and install after restart"
Once the download of all the plugins
have finished click "Restart Jenkins".
If, after a few minutes, Jenkins does not restart
return back to the main page for Jenkins
then manually reload your browser.
Great. Once the page comes back up Jenkins is back alive.
Now that we've downloaded and installed the plugins for
PMD, Findbugs, and Checkstyle let's
enable them for our project.
Click on the time tracker project.
Once that loads click on configure.
Scroll down until you find build settings.
You should have three new items "Publish Checkstyle
analysis results", the same for Findbugs, and for PMD.
We're going to check all three.
Each one of these has its own additional
advanced settings, we'll cover that later.
For now click on apply.
Before we can run our build and
expect the quality metrics to show up
we do need to change the Maven command being used.
Jump to the build section.
For goals and options we should have "clean package".
The clean goal does what you would expect; it clears
out the temporary files from a previous build.
"package" runs the default lifecycle up to the package phase,
which will compile the code and put
the results into a jar or war file.
Unit testing will happen here as well.
There are a couple ways we could add code quality analysis.
The first way is to simply add the
"site" target to our Maven command.
That will trigger the analysis plugin while the site plugin
attempts to build and HTML report for all the findings.
Basically it would run the analysis
after all the parts of the build.
The other approach is to specify the plugin go for
each of the analysis tools we're going to use .
Using this approach we'll run the analysis
more in line with the rest of the build.
Let's use that approach, since I really don't
need the HTML report provided by the "site" goal.
After the other two goals specify
"checkstyle:checkstyle pmd:pmd findbugs:findbugs"
The first half is the short name for the
plugin "checkstyle", "pmd" and "findbugs".
Maven knows how to resolve this to the full names,
including the most current versions of those plugins.
The second half is the actual plugin goal to execute.
In each case the main goal to run happens
to be named after the plugin or tool.
I've tested out both approaches
and they give identical results.
While just using the cycle is less typing,
specifying each plugin and plugin goal
makes it clear to me what's going on.
We need either approach in order to
activate the analysis reports in Jenkins.
Alright, now that we've done that, click on Save,
which will return us to the project status page.
In this lesson we made sure the JUnit plugin was
already installed and then we installed PMD,
Findbugs and Checkstyle plugins so that we can evaluate
the project source code quality in this section.
Then we configured the time tracker
project to use the code quality plugins.
In the next lesson we will run the
build and review the results.
