--- Reviewing Quality Analysis ---

Welcome, in this lesson, we are going to
review the code quality reports in our project
so we know what changes are needed to achieve
a high quality, bug-free software project.
Back on the time tracker project status page, we
have our trend reports racking up data points
but since nothing has changed the
reports have remained the same.
Let's review the reports before tweaking the code.
Like before, let's dive into the
console output of the last build.
Search for "checkstyle" in the output of your browser.
This should jump down to where the
analysis is run for the project.
Here we have a few log messages that start with
the tool, "checkstyle", "pmd", and "findbugs".
You can see where each plugin runs within each module
and provides a summary of the findings after analysis.
While we are here at the build level
check out the build status page.
Here we have the test results and the code quality
results for Checkstyle, PMD, and Findbugs,
with a summary of the results and a link to the actual report.
Also you should notice that we have
those same links on the side menu.
Next we will look at each report and their violation findings.
While each tool has its own main purpose and strength
some types of violations will be repeated in each tool.
For example both Checkstyle and
PMD will find empty code blocks.
Let's look at the Checkstyle report first.
Checkstyle is a common coding style
analysis tool used for Java projects.
It checks against industry standard ways of coding
by default, but your team can define its own rules
and adjust the priority for the existing
ones if you find that necessary.
This, like any style guide, is a suite of strong
opinions that everyone more or less agrees to follow.
Click on the "Checkstyle Warnings" side menu
item to go into that report for this build..
Once on the Checkstyle result page review the warnings.
There won't be any new or fixed warnings,
since those compare changes between builds
and we haven't made any code changes just yet.
Under summary we have a lot of high priority
warnings and no findings for normal or low priority.
Under details we see where our warnings
are located by the following: "packages".
This is the way Java projects are grouped
together, like namespaces in other languages.
In this case we have two packages
that contain Checkstyle violations.
Files lists specific files where
violations are found with a total by file.
Categories, which lists the violation by Checkstyle category,
like design, Java dock use or lack
thereof, or whitespace issues.
Types lists violations by specific Checkstyle check type,
like checking for empty blocks or issues with braces.
You can think of this as a refinement of the categories.
Warnings lists out all the
violations and their location within
each file, package location,
priority level, type, and category.
Origin attempts to track down how
the violations were introduced.
Details lists each violation along with stats and
description of the violation and why it matters,
granted some descriptions are better than others.
OK, before heading back, let's poke around this report.
As we drill down we just refine the context of the report.
Also, because of the tight Jenkins integration, the breadcrumb
trail is updated to reflect our place within the report.
Eventually we will drill down to a
specific violation within the code.
If you hover over your mouse over the highlighted line Jenkins
will provide a pop up detailing the specific violation,
which can be a bit crazy depending on how verbose
the violations description happens to be.
Anyway, I think you get the point.
For now let's head back to review the other reports.
From the current page click on PMD warnings to see
that report for the current build of this project.
PMD is a static code analysis tool that focuses
on the Java ecosystem, since it supports Java
and languages and technologies commonly used
within Java projects, like JSP and XML.
Instead of focusing on programming style,
PMD looks for bad coding practices,
like empty code blocks, code that can never be
reached, problematic syntax, and code duplication.
Alrght, back on the report.
We can see the report looks very similar to
the Checkstyle report we just walked through.
Because of that I'm not going to spend too
much time going over the same type of report.
Of course the findings or violations will be
different, but the overall concept is the same.
You can drill down in various ways
with summaries at each level.
Eventually you'll end up looking at a
specific violation at the file level.
Since this is a different report
showing different types of violations
do spend a few moments becoming familiar with this report.
Once done browsing the PMD report click on the Findbugs
warnings side menu item to jump to that report for this build.
Findbugs is a static code analysis tool that
looks for actual bugs or mistakes in Java code.
Other tools focus more on coding style or
questionable code that might be buggy,
but Findbugs focuses on code that looks just plain wrong.
Because of that I have much fewer violations in this report;
in this case, I only have two warnings, one
high priority and one normal priority.
Like the other reports we have a similar report style
provided by Jenkins, which makes getting around pretty easy.
Let's drill down into the high
priority issue, eventually landing
on the specific line in the file
where the violation exists.
Like before, if I hover my mouse over the highlighted line
I'll get a pop up with more details
on the violation and warning.
Well that's it regarding the reports.
As we saw they all follow a similar look and feel, so
navigating around different reports is easy and familiar.
Let's jump back to the main status page for the entire project.
Once here I want to point out the same
set of side menu items available here.
If you click on them they will take you to
the corresponding report of the last build.
Also available on the project's status page you can click on
the specific build data point for each of the trend reports
to jump directly to that report for that specific build,
which can be a visual way to jump directly
to builds with unusual dips or spikes.

reports generated and gathered by Jenkins

to gather those stats into reports easily browsed

--- Enforcing Minimum Quality Scores ---

Welcome, in this video, we're going to set
some threshold in order to fail our build,
and then later make our build unstable.
However, before we do that, let's install one more plugin.
With our Maven project in Jenkins we ran
our build with quality metrics enabled.
However, that's just giving us nice line graphs but
not really doing anything regarding our build.
For me the main benefit of having quality metrics
built into Jenkins is not necessarily for reporting,
although that is useful, but mainly for evaluating the quality
of the build in order for it to proceed forward or not.
Let's have Jenkins take some action based on
the quality of the code within our project.
While it is possible to set thresholds
for each static code analysis plugin,
I have found that setting thresholds for more
than one tool conflicts with the others.
Additionally I generally only care
about the aggregate number of warnings
and determining the quality thresholds based on that.
To collect all the static code analysis together I like using
the static analysis collector plugin to gather all the results
from PMD, Findbugs, and Checkstyle, and make the
evaluation on the project quality based on that.
Go to the plugin manager,
and once there click on the available tab.
Then, in the search filter field, type analysis.
Then find and install the analysis collector plugin.
Once the plugin has been installed
return to the Jenkins dashboard.
Now we need to enable the plugin within our project.
Return to the configure page for the time tracker project.
Jump down to the "Build Settings" section.
We will keep the checkmarks next to the publish
analysis reports for Checkstyle, Findbugs, and PMD.
That will allow us to feed the new
plugin with our analysis reports.
Now, under the "Post-build Actions" section,
add a new post build action and choose
"Publish combined static analysis results".
The "Post-build Section" is used to run extra steps or actions
after the main build steps that normally run
regardless of the overall build status.
Now just make sure that all three items are checked:
Checkstyle warnings, Findbugs warnings, and PMD warnings.
If we had other code quality analysis tools
here that were compatible with this plugin,
more options would be listed here.
For now that's all we need, click save
and return to the project's status page.
Let's build again and check out the results.
Great, we should have a similar result from last time, but
now we have more information on the project status page;
a summary of the three static code analysis
tools under the heading "Analysis results",
with links to specific results in their respective reports.
We also have a "Static Analysis Warnings" item
in the side menu that provides a combined report
from all the analysis tools enabled for this report.
Clicking on the latest build on the
build status page we see a new item:
static analysis warnings with a summary
and linked to the report from here.
And, of course, the corresponding side menu item as well.
Jump back to the project status page.
If we run the build one more time, which
gives us two build with this plugin enabled,
the project status page will include a
combined static analysis trend graph.
Now lets configure the new combined static analysis
plugin to enforce certain quality gates for our project.
Click on configure, and jump down to the "Post-build Actions".
And, for the post-build action
here, we have an advanced button.
Click on "Advanced", and under the
advanced settings we have several options.
The status thresholds is where we can
tell whether or not to fail the build
or to make it unstable based on the number of issues detected.
Near the top we have something called
"Health thresholds", which will provide
some type of weather status based on the
number of violations that are detected.
I'll consider things wonderful if
we have less than five warnings,
and bad thundercloud status if we go beyond 50 warnings.
Next we have the "Health priorities",
which determines which warning priorities we
consider for evaluating the health thresholds above.
Finally we have a matrix for status thresholds.
Here we have four columns: we have all priorities,
high priorities, normal, and low, with two rows.
The top row, with the yellow ball, refers to
thresholds to consider the build unstable.
The bottom row is the threshold to fail the build.
For example, on this project, I
want to consider the build unstable
if we have more than 10 warnings, regardless of the priority.
Placing a zero in the high priority column will trip the
unstable status if one or more high priority warnings is found.
I place a 5 for normal and 10 for low, making these
unstable thresholds for those categories respectively.
That means, provided we don't exceed 10 warnings total,
the project can have up to five normal priority
warnings and still get a success or green ball.
Let's pick some reasonable thresholds for
failing the build, which is the bottom row.
For all priorities let's use 100. For high, normal,
and low use 40, 75, and blank respectively.
That means, if the project has more than 40 high priority
warnings or more than 75 normal priority warnings
Jenkins will automatically fail the build, which will
also happen if any combination exceeds 100 warnings.
The last field, for low priority warnings, is left blank
which means low priority
warnings will not cause a build
failure, provided we don't exceed
the number for all priorities.
Great, that's it for now, click save to save the
configuration and return to the project status page.
We just configured the thresholds that will
cause a build failure, which is intentional.
Click "Build Now" and see if we caused a failing build.
We had the same information on the project status
page, but if we click into the specific build,
on the build status page if we look at the static analysis
summary which tells us we exceeded one of our thresholds,
which is why the build is marked as failed.
We can also see this if we go to
the console output for this build,
and go all the way to the end.
We see, although the Maven part of the build was successful,
since the static collector plugin evaluated the thresholds
in turn the plugin actually changed the build status
to failure, because of the missed quality threshold.
For now let's return to the project's status page.
In this lesson we installed a new plugin that
aggregates our static code analysis reports
and allows us to set the thresholds or quality gates
upon the aggregate number of warnings by priority.
In the next lesson we will make
changes to the project source code
in an attempt to pass the thresholds and
improve the quality of the project.

--- Publishing Overview ---

Welcome, in this section, we are going to turn
Jenkins into a Maven artifact repository.
so other systems or processes can access our build artifacts.
To be fair this isn't something I would consider a long
term solution for archiving artifacts indefinitely,
but instead an easy way to provide an
artifact repository for recent builds
without having to set up a completely
separate software program for that purpose.
If your team or company needs a fully
featured Maven or artifact repository
consider setting up Sonatype Nexus,
Artifactory, or Apache Archiva.
However, since this course is focused on Jenkins,
let's move forward with the Jenkins only approach.
To turn Jenkins into a Maven repository
we simply need to download and install a special
plugin called the Maven repository server plugin.
Then, after that, we will need to configure our Maven projects
to use Jenkins as a Maven repository when each build completes.
Any new build's artifacts will be available
like a standard Maven repository.
Great, let's get started.

--- Maven Repository Plugin and Configuration ---

Welcome, in this video, I'm going to install the plug
in for creating a Maven repository within Jenkins.
Within Jenkins go to "Manage
Jenkins", then go to Manage Plugins.
Go to the available tab.
On filter type "maven".
Then scroll down until you find the
"Maven Repository Server" plugin.
Check that box and then click on "Download
now and install after restart".
Check the box to restart Jenkins.
Alright, once that's done, go back to the dashboard.
Remember our restart trick if you need it.
With the Maven repository server plugin installed
you should have a new menu item under the left
hand side side menu called "Maven Repository".
Now that we have the plugin installed
let's configure our project to use it.
However, before we can do that we need to
make one small change to our Maven project.
The Maven repository server plugin
requires a web friendly project name
in order to work correctly at the project level.
We've already gone through this process earlier, so I
will walk through it again, a bit faster this time.
Within the time tracker project go into configure.
I'm going to copy the current name, which we
all use as a display name in just a moment.
Now I'm going to rename the project
to "time-tracker", all lowercase.
Notice that I use all lowercase and dashes the separate words.
Save and confirm the changes.
Now, with the project renamed, let's add the display name.
Go to configure.
Then, under advanced, paste in the value from
our clipboard or just type in: "Time Tracker".
This change can be done alongside other changes, so
click Apply to save and continue with other changes.
Scroll down until you find the build environment section.
You should have an item that says "Define
Upstream Maven Repository". Check that box.
When that box is checked you'll have some additional
parameters that you will need to configure.
Upstream type will be project. Project
should be this one, using the new name.
Build should be "Last Successful",
and leave "Promoted Build" empty.
Now that we've done that click save.
Now we should be back on the project status page once again.
In this lesson we installed the Maven repository server
plugin and configured our project to use the plugin,
which we will do in the next lesson
when we build the updated project..

--- Deployment Overview ---

Welcome, in this section, we will download, install,
configure, and deploy to the Apache Tomcat application server
since Tomcat is the most popular Java web
application server currently available.
In doing so we will configure our Jenkins Maven
project to deploy automatically to Tomcat
once the application server is up and running,
which will require installing a new plugin.
This will finish the overall pipeline for
Java projects by including deployment.
Of course, the entire pipeline or
workflow has been very simple.
In your case you might want to add more complete testing
or additional testing steps before automatically
deploying an artifact to a live deployment environment.
Or perhaps you will only allow automatic
deployments to non-production environments
and set up a more manual, approval
driven, production deployment system.
Either way this could be done completely within Jenkins.
The first part of this section will deal
with installation and configuration.
Since this core supports several operating systems,
I have included different installation videos
that focus on Windows Mac and Linux respectively
since the installation process is
rather different on each platform.
If you only care about your operating system
just watch that video and skip the others.
Additionally, since both Jenkins and Tomcat
use port 8080 as their default listening port
there is a bit more set up needed to allow both Jenkins
and Tomcat to run on the same system at the same time.

--- Deployment Plugin ---

Welcome, in this lesson, we're

that refreshes your browser with Jenkins.

Alright, now that we have the required plugin
installed, let's hook up our Jenkins project to Tomcat.
For the time tracker project click on configure,
then go to the post build actions, then
select "Deploy war/ear to a container".
Make sure that this item is set up
after the analysis post-build action;
we don't want to bother deploying something
that is so bad that it causes a build failure.
Also, in this context, the term "container"
simply means a web application server
not containerization technology like Docker.
I just want to make sure that was clear.
Within this action for "WAR/EAR files", you can
use an Ant style path for example "**/*.war",
which will pick up any "war" file within our project.
For context path type "/demo".
Under containers go to "Add Container". Select "Tomcat 8.x".
At least as of the recording of this course, the plug
in hasn't been updated to list Tomcat 9 just yet.
However, not much has changed from Tomcat 8
and this plugin will work equally as well.
The Tomcat URL is "http://localhost:9080"
Return to "credentials". Click the "Add"
button to use the credentials manager
to set up a new username and password
configuration to use with this server.
In the dropdown menu select Jenkins.
With the Jenkins credentials provider open
all the pre-filled items should be correct;
Domain should be "Global", kind should be "Username
with password", and scope should be "Global" as well.
Now specify Jenkins for the username and password fields.
For ID this is a unique identifier used internally
by Jenkins.Type "tomcat-manager-scripts".
The description can be anything, but I recommend something
useful to remind yourself about these credentials.
I'm using "Login username and password
for local Tomcat server script manager".
Once all that is done, click "Add".
Now, back to the configuration page.
Under "Tomcat 8.x" credentials there should be a dropdown
containing the item we just created. Select that item.
Once that's all set up, click save, which
returns us to the project status page.
Before moving on I need to make one correction.
I mentioned earlier the deploy step we just
configured should go after the analysis step.
The reverse is actually correct, since the deploy
plugin does not understand the unstable build status.
Of course, there are several ways to address this;
one approach is to simply relax the analysis rules just a
bit to allow more issues to not flag the build as unstable.
We could leave this alone and take a hard line
for deployments, or what I'm going to do now;
simply flip the order to allow the deploy step to
run first, based on the build status at that point.
When the analysis is evaluated afterwards,
it won't affect the deployment step at all.
To do that simply drag and drop the deploy
step above the analysis step, like this.
Once everything is done, click save, which
returns us to the project status page.
In this lesson we installed the deploy to container plugin
needed to deploy successful build artifacts to Tomcat,
and then we configured our project to
use that plugin in a post build action.

--- Deployment ---

Welcome, in this lesson, we're going to

should see a "/demo" running on context "demo".

--- Security Overview ---

Welcome, in this section, we will dive
into security within Jenkins a bit more,
since no course on Jenkins would be complete
without a discussion about security.
Since Jenkins 2, there has been a set up wizard the first
time someone attempts to access the Jenkins dashboard.
That process guides users to set up an initial
admin account and also security by default.
This is a major improvement over previous versions
of Jenkins when security was disabled by default,
and everyone accessing Jenkins basically had full admin rights.
While a secure by default approach is great,
there is so much more to Jenkins security,
and we will cover many of those options during this section.
To be fair, security is a vast topic and it is not limited
to configuring users or managing permissions within Jenkins.
In this section we will cover the following:
Manually disabling security; this is a safeguard,
just in case we lock ourselves out of Jenkins
which is easy to do while modifying security settings.
Then we will re-enable security, and
look at some basic security options.
We will manage permissions using the
built in, matrix based security options.
Finally we will become even more fine grained with role
based security, which requires installing a plugin.
After this section you should have a strong foundation
in the major ways to enable and manage
users and permissions within Jenkins.
Great, let's get started with a trick
that just might save your bacon.

--- Manually Disable Security ---

Welcome, in this video, we're going to
edit the Jenkins configuration file
to disable security as a means of last resort
if you happen to get locked out of Jenkins.
Whenever you mess around with security settings there's
always a possibility of locking yourself out of Jenkins.
If you do that, there aren't many ways to recover
Your options are reinstall Jenkins,
which is often not a viable option,
or to manually disable security in Jenkins by
directly editing the main configuration file;
that's the option I'm going to show you now.
I'll be using Atom as my text editor.
I'm going to open Atom using my desktop icon, right
clicking on the icon, and selecting "Run as Administrator",
which will allow me to edit files my logged
in user normally it does not have access to.
On Mac and Linux I recommend using the terminal
to open Atom by using the sudo command,
which will run Atom as the root user.
When Atom opens, If there is anything open
from a previous session, close that out now.
I'm going to "Add Project Folder",
and navigate until I find the Jenkins
installation folder on my system
With the Jenkins folder open in my project
tree panel click on the "config.xml" file,
which will open in a new panel
containing the contents of that file.
This is the primary configuration file for Jenkins.
There are actually several other configuration files for
Jenkins, which are mainly located in the same directory
but this is the main configuration file, which
contains many of the most important settings.
In particular we are looking for the "useSecurity"
tag, which turns on and off the security in Jenkins.
In this case it is near the top of the
file, just a few lines down from the top.
After that tag we also have the "authorizationStrategy"
tag and the "securityRelm" tag.
Let's use the standard XML comment
syntax to comment out these two tags.
Since my editor understands XML syntax, my display updates
accordingly as I comment out the security related tags.
Now, return to the "useSecurity" tag.
Change the value to false, and then save the file.
As long as you opened Atom as the administrator or the
root user the file should save without any problems.
Once the configuration file is saved,
you will need to restart Jenkins
using your system's service or daemon command, since I
will assume you are completely locked out of Jenkins.
After restarting Jenkins, return to the dashboard.
At this point we have completely
removed security from Jenkins..
In this lesson we manually disabled security in Jenkins
by making edits directly on the main configuration file
for Jenkins, and then restarting the Jenkins server.
While it is nice that we can do this we need to enable some
basic level of security, which we will do in the next lesson.

--- Enable Security and Login ---

This controls where Jenkins looks for

For those unfamiliar with managing security and
access there are two parts that come together;
authentication, which challenges
the user to prove their identity,
and authorization, which controls what
access the authenticated user has.
With that, under authorization, let's change
to "Logged-in users can do anything",
which is slightly more secure than anyone can do anything.
When we select that option we are given the
option to allow anonymous read access,
which does what you think and I'm OK with
that, So I will keep that item checked

--- Adding Users in Matrix-Based Security ---

Welcome, in this video, we're going
to set up matrix-based security.
Return to the global security page to
review our security and access options.
Right now the security realm is set up to use
the Jenkins own user database, which is fine.
Other options are much more advanced.
A plugin we will use later will help us address that need,
but if you're running Jenkins in a corporate environment
you might want to explore LDAP or Active Directory Integration.
Under authorization, we have several options available to us.
The more interesting ones would be the "Matrix-based
security" and the "Project-based Matrix Security".
These provide more opportunity to refine the
security model, and they are both closely related.
Let's start with the Matrix-based
security; click on that option.
In doing so, we have a fairly wide
table that expands below that option.
This table provides a matrix of
permissions based on users and groups.
By default you get two groups: anonymous
users and authenticated users.
You could probably guess what these two groups represent.
Long wise we have each of their permissions we can
change or manage grouped into several categories.
For example the overall category represents
the global, sweeping permissions.
The permissions become more specific
as we move across the table.
At the very end we have two small buttons that allow
setting or unsetting all the items for a specific row.
Return to the start of the table. We have
access to a field to add users and groups.
As I've mentioned before, groups aren't
supported by the internal Jenkins database,
but we can add the users we've already created.
I'll start with the admin account that
we set up at the start of the course.
Type the username in the field and click add.
A new row will show up under the two
preexisting groups for the newly added user,
displaying the full name of the account.
Let's add the other account we just created.
Now we can set the specific permissions by group and by user

This will provide some basic, read-only,

Additionally, click "Discover", "Read",
and "Workspace" permissions under "Job".
And finally "Read" under "Review".
You can hover your mouse over each permission for
more information about that particular item.
The same is true for each of the
specific checkboxes within the table,
which can be handy as the table grows with many users.
For the other groups and users let's take advantage of
the shortcut buttons on the far end of the table rows.
Click the checkmark image for the second and third rows.
The fourth row is my normal user account;
I'm just going to use the same permissions
as the other normal authenticated users
To remove a user click on the red
X, which I do for the last row.
Now, let's refine the permissions a bit.
Of course, we need to provide some distinction
between the Admin account and everyone else.
Uncheck the following permissions for the
second row: "Administer" under "Overall",
all permissions under "Credentials", and all
permissions except for "Build" under "Agent".
Authenticated users still have a lot of permissions,
but simply don't have the rights to
change anything under Manage Jenkins.
Double check the permissions table and make sure at least
one user has admin rights, or all permissions checked.
This is one place where it is super
easy to lock yourself out of Jenkins.
If that happens, you'll have to use the manual configuration
trick to disable security to allow yourself back into Jenkins.
Assuming everything looks good,
click "Save" to save the changes.
Like we've done before let's confirm the expected
access for non-logged in users by logging out.
Return to the Jenkins dashboard, which should
display the jobs we have set up so far.
And, like before, you won't have access to trigger builds,
make configuration changes, or of course Manage Jenkins.
Take a few moments to poke around
and see how things are different.
Until now, we really haven't had a distinction
between a normal user and the admin user
we created at the start of the course.
With the matrix based security we
now have that difference in place.
We can see that by logging into Jenkins using the normal
or non-admin user account we created earlier in this section.
Once logged in we have more permissions to do things
in Jenkins, like trigger builds or configure jobs,

Again, poke around to notice any
additional rights for authenticated users.
Now let's log back in as the admin account.
Once logged in the "Manage Jenkins" option is
back and we can do anything in Jenkins again.
Let's return to the security page for one last change.

security at the project level.
When we select that option we are presented with the same

provide the default or system wide permissions
which can be overwritten at the project level.
Replicate the permissions we set up earlier.
Once that's done let's modify the
permissions for authenticated users.

Great. We just set up the defaults and system wide permissions.

--- Users Role ---

Welcome, in this video, we're going to change over
to the role security authentication for fine grained
So far, we've enabled to security and explored
increasing levels of better security.
In order to take things further,
we need to install another plugin.
Go to "Manage Plugins",
and then go to the available tab.
Within the filter search field type "role".
Find the "Role-based Authorization Strategy".
Check that item and then click on the button
for "Download now and install after restart".
While here we'll also check the box to restart Jenkins.
Once Jenkins has finished restarting, log in if necessary.
In doing so, make sure you log in as the admin account.
If you automatically logged in as your other account
log out, and then re-login as the admin account.
Once logged in go to "Manage Jenkins", and
then click on "Configure Global Security".
Let's verify our plugin was successfully installed;
under authorization, we should now have
an option for "Role-Based Strategy".
Now that we verified that the "Role-Based
Strategy" plugin has been enabled
on the configure global security page, under the
authorization section, select "Role-based Strategy".
Once you've done that click save.
Once you return to the "Manage Jenkins" page
scroll down and we should now have a new
item called "Manage and Assign Roles".
Click on that item.
This is where we manage roles and assign users to those roles.
Click on "Manage Roles".
We have three categories of roles: "Global
roles", "Project roles", and "Slave roles".
We'll be concerning ourselves with global and
project roles, starting off with global roles
I like to think of roles as a type of group
of users with the same access rights.
Doing so overcomes the lack of groups while using
the Jenkins internal database for authentication.
By default we're given a single roll called admin that
has all the permissions and there are no project roles.
Use the "Role to add" field to add two more global roles.
First let's add a roll called users, which will represent
authenticated users from the previous security models.
Type in "users" and click on the "Add" button.
Great, now let's add the other role called guests, which
will be for non-logged in users, that is anonymous users.
As we add each new role, the
permissions table will expand a bit.
The permissions table is basically the same table that
we've seen in setting up the matrix security earlier.
If you need a reminder of what something means simply
hover your mouse over that item for some help text.
For the "users” role check the following items:
"Read" under "Overview"; "View" under "Credentials";
"Build" under "Job"; "Build" under "Agent";
"Cancel", "Discover", "Read", and "Workspace"
under "Job"; "Update" under "Run";
"Read" under "Review"; and "Tag" under "SCM"
I realize that's all very specific; basically I want the user's
role to be able to trigger builds and review the results
without being able to make significant changes
to the builds or the Jenkins configuration.
For the "guests" role, which will be much
more restrictive, check the following items:
"Read" under "Overview"; "Discover", "Read", and
"Workspace" under job; and "Read" under "View".
The guest role will provide read-only access
to Jenkins and all the jobs defined within it.
Take a few moments to review the
configuration of the new roles.
Make sure you leave the admin role with all checkboxes
or you risk locking yourself out of Jenkins.
Of course, the permissions I'm setting up could be anything;
you may choose more or less restrictive permissions.
Once content with the new roles, we need the save our changes.
Go to the bottom of the page and click on the save button.
This should return us to the "Manage and Assign Roles" page.
Now that we have our global roles defined,
we need to assign users to those roles.
To do that, go into the "Assign Roles" page.
Here we have the three roles we defined
earlier: "admin", "users" and "guests".
We will also have a list of users and
groups we can assign to those roles.
By default the logged in account that enabled
role-based security will be granted the admin role.
This, of course, can be changed
later, but for now this is fine.
This is also the reason why I wanted you to log
in as the Admin User for this part of the video.
Add the other user account, that is the non-admin
account, using the "User/group to add" field.
Once you have type in your user's name click "Add".
Your other account name should appear in the permissions table.
Now assign that user to the user's role.
I'll also assign the provided
"Anonymous" group to the guest role.
Once that's done review the changes.
Confirm the admin account is assigned to the admin role.
Now click the save button at the bottom of the page.
We will, again, return to the "Assign and Manage Roles" page.
The permissions changes should take effect immediately.
Let's start by logging out.
Once logged out, the permissions we will be
assigned to will be the global guests role,
in this case we can see our jobs in a read-only mode.
Of course, we can't trigger any builds or manage Jenkins.
This is very similar to the other anonymous
access that we have in other security approaches.
Now, let's log in as the non-admin user.
Once logged in, the permissions of
the user's role will be applied,
which will allow us to trigger builds on all the jobs.
However we aren't able to configure, delete, or create new
jobs, nor are we able to do anything management related.
Finally, log back in as the admin user.
We should be back to the full permissions.
In this lesson we installed and enabled
the role-based strategy plugin
in order to provide finer level of security within Jenkins
We created a couple new roles and
assigned our users to those roles,
and then verified the access to Jenkins afterwards.

--- Project Role ---

Welcome, in this lesson, we will set up the
role-based security at the project level.
For this video I want to set up a couple of freestyle projects
From the Jenkins dashboard click on "New Item".
For the project name I want to use a specific convention,
basically prefixing a team name and
then the name of the software project.
Let's call it "awesome-client"; again all
lowercases with dashes to separate words.
Select "Freestyle project" and then click "OK".
For now I'm not going to configure anything, so just "Save".
I want to create a couple more projects
using "awesome" as the project name prefix.
Follow the same steps to create freestyle projects
named "awesome-service" and awesome-library.
You can set up other projects that follow
the same pattern if you're really up for it
Once done creating all the projects, return to the
Jenkins dashboard where all the projects will be listed.
Now that we've added several other projects,
let's refine the role security to allow a normal
user to manage all the "awesome" projects.
While logged in as the admin user, return to "Manage Roles"
under "Manage and Assign Roles" within "Manage Jenkins".
From the global section our non-admin account has the
rights or permissions granted by the user's role,
which isn't enough for the "awesome" projects.
For those that user should be able to make
configuration changes or any other types of changes.
We only want to apply the elevated rights
to the "awesome" projects, and no others.
Jump down to the project roles.
We need to add a role and a pattern for that role.
The "Role to add" field is the name of the roll.
I recommend using a name like the project
name plus the access level for the role.
The pattern is a regular expression that will match against
the project or job names for inclusion in the role.
This is one reason that all the new
project use the prefix "awesome" in them.
For "Role to add" I'm using "awesome-admin", since this role
will effectively Grant admin rights to the awesome jobs.
For the pattern I'm using this expression: up carrot;
the literal text "awesome-"; period; then asterisk.
Then click "Add" to add the project roll.
Once the new roll appears in the table just check all the items
and click "Apply" at the bottom of the
page to save our progress so far.
Great, now let's dig in to the regular expression we just used.
Regular expressions are notoriously complex and often
require a detailed breakdown of what's going on.
I recommend using an online tool, or an application
to help with composing regular expressions.
One web site that I have found very useful is "regex101.com".
Open it up in a new web browser tab.
Let's copy the expression we just used and paste it
into the regular expression field in the other tab.
Then, in the test string field, type the name
of one of the projects: "awesome-client"
We should have one match detected.
You can hover your mouse over each of the
characters in the regular expression field
for an explanation of what each character does.
The highlighted characters have special meaning
and the other characters are literals.
The expression breaks down like this:
the leading carrot sets the start position for the
pattern; then the literal text of "awesome-";
finally ending with a dot, which matches any character;
followed by an asterisk, which means zero or more times.
This combination effectively matches
any remaining part of the string.
Basically, this pattern will match any
project name that starts with "awesome-".
Any regular expressions can be
complex, but they are very powerful.
For now let's return to Jenkins.
Let's finalize our changes by clicking save,
which saves the changes and returns
us to the manage and assign roles.
Let's return to the "Assign Roles" page and apply the new role.
Go down to the "Item roles" and then
add the name of the non-admin account.
Once the user is added check the
item under the name "awesome-admin",
which is the name of the project level role we created earlier.
Once done click "Save".
Now that we have created our project level role and
assigned a user to that role we need to test it out.
Since we are currently logged in as the admin
user, log out and log back in as the other user.
Once logged in as the other user, from the Jenkins dashboard,
you should have access to build and view any project.
However, you should have additional access
to all the projects that start with awesome.
Click on any one of them, and confirm that
you have access to the configure page.
Return to the Jenkins dashboard, and then
do the same for the time tracker project.
This time, like before, you don't have access to the configure
page since that option is not listed in the side menu.
In this lesson we demonstrated the use of the
role strategy plugin to provide additional power
in managing groups of projects with
the power of regular expressions.

--- Jenkins Production Using Lightsail Overview ---

Welcome, in this section, we are going to move
into a more production style setup with Jenkins
by implementing a Jenkins server hosted in the cloud.
There are several reasons for moving into the cloud.
We first started off with a local installation of Jenkins,
which is certainly one of the easiest and
definitely the cheapest ways to get to know Jenkins.
However, at some point you have to move beyond
the local system in order for others to benefit.
That brings us to this section,
where I will walk you through how to setup Jenkins
in a production style environment using Amazon Lightsail.
In doing so we will set up Jenkins
in an Internet accessible manner,
which opens up new abilities and features that
simply wouldn't work while remaining local.
While considering a home for Jenkins as the backbone
of your CI/CD infrastructure there are many options.
One is the traditional self-hosting
or on premise hosting, either
under a desk in the office or
in your company's datacenter.
You might be required to go this route for various reasons.
More commonly companies are moving to the cloud.
The cloud is just another way of
saying someone else's datacenter.
That's it; there's nothing fancy about it.
In fact, companies have been using cloud
computing before it was called the cloud.
We had co-location hosting, where companies rented
space for their own hardware in a data center.
Then we had dedicated server hosting,
where companies would rent existing hardware in a datacenter.
Then virtualization made it possible to
rent only a small part of that hardware,
which has grown into infrastructure as a service or IaaS,
with companies providing fancy web-based
management of virtualized infrastructure.
What we think of the main cloud hosting providers of
today, that is Amazon, Microsoft, and Google,
are just refinements and enhancements to the IaaS offerings
by providing an ever expanding list of services
beyond the simple virtual machine hosting.
However, with added abilities comes with it
increased complexity, especially around billing.
That's where Amazon Lightsail comes into play.
This course is primarily about Jenkins and CI/CD processes,
so I don't want to spend too much time going into
the complexities of a major cloud hosting provider
just to stand up a Jenkins server and related services.
I looked at Digital Ocean, Linode, or other virtual private
server hosting or infrastructure as a service providers.
Most don't provide a free trial period, even though
most services are relatively inexpensive these days.
Lightsail offers inexpensive consistent hosting services
after an initial free one month trial with all the simplified
collections of services backed by Amazon AWS resources,
just without the complexity and the huge
learning curve normally associated with AWS.
That allows us to focus on Jenkins instead of the platform.
Of course you will need to learn about
Lightsail, but I will guide you through it
and I won't be skipping the required
steps in order to oversimplify it.
We don't have to; Amazon takes care of all
the details without making us less secure.
Of course, there are tradeoffs.
Lightsail is really like having a virtual private server
hosting offered by Amazon instead of other hosting providers
which means Lightsail would be a good fit for
hosting needs that would normally go into a VPS,
like Linote or Digital Ocean.
Like other VPS providers Lightsail provides a
handful of pre-configured template applications,
like WordPress, Joomla, or a LampStack server,
or you can select from an even smaller list
of operating system only starting points.
These offerings can be a little out of date; for example,
right now, AWS Lightsail offers the prior LTS release of Ubuntu
although the current LTS release has been
publicly available for over six months.
Also, while Lightsail uses standard
AWS resources under the hood,
none of those resources will appear in your normal AWS account.
Everything in AWS Lightsail is managed
entirely within a separate web interface,
which is separate from the normal AWS console.
However, it is possible to use AWS resources directly
within Lightsail by using a special link called VPC peering,
which gives us a way to start out
in Lightsail and grow accordingly.
Since I will be walking you through all the steps you can
think of this section as a mini-course about AWS Lightsail
in addition to how to set up Jenkins in a cloud environment.
One last thing I need to mention,
since I will be using AWS starting in this section
you might not be able to follow along if
you are not in a country serviced by AWS.
Also some services I will be using won't be free.
I'll do my best to stay within the free tier,
or to use services included during the free month trial period
but not all services qualify and I'm not going
to hold back just because they aren't free.
If you aren't able to follow along, either because
of geography or finances, I sincerely apologize.
But many of the concepts covered are
still worth watching and taking notes.
Now that we understand what we're getting into
let's dive in by setting up an AWS account.

already covered will be the Nginx web server,
which will act as a reverse proxy.
The first installation script will
be the "jenkins-master.sh" file.
Click on that file to open it.
The first line of the script, called the shebang,
tells the shell environment which program or language to use
to read and execute the rest of the script.
In this case this is a bash script.
The first several script lines
use the "apt-get" package manager
to install several utilities
including Git and open JDK.
The "-y" option allows us to automatically pass the acceptance
prompts, otherwise the script would hang at that point.
Then the script will download and install Maven.
I will use a variable to hold the specific version of
Maven so I only have to change it in one location later
The next few lines are the slight variation of the script lines
taken from the Jenkins installation instructions
for Debian-based Linux distributions.
Instead of updating the main apt repository file,
I created a dedicated one for Jenkins instead.
After all that we installed Jenkins using "apt-get"
and then make sure the Jenkins Damon
is enabled and running on our system.
All this so far is really nothing new, perhaps even better
scripted out than the first time around, but that's about it.
The rest of the script will install the Nginx web server and
download a configuration file, which will look at after this.
By default Jenkins runs on port
8080, which we have seen before.
While running locally this isn't a major issue,
however once we put Jenkins on the open Internet
I want to put Jenkins behind a proxy server that is
only accessible via the normal web site port of 80.
This limits our exposure, since that is the normal port
open and it won't require making any firewall changes.
Like we did with Jenkins the last few lines
will enable and start the Nginx daemon.
Now return to the Lightsail folder and
click on the "lightsail-jenkins.conf" file.
That will be the nginx configuration needed to
set up a reverse proxy to the Jenkins server.
Nginx will listen on port 80 and pass along
all traffic to port 8080 on the local host.
That's where Jenkins will be running.
There are a few instructions here to help nginx
with the reverse proxy, like setting some headers.
Once done reviewing this file, return to lightsail folder.
Here we have a ReadMe file with
instructions on how to set up a KeyPair.
We will follow these instructions soon in an upcoming lesson.
In this lesson I walked through
the script and configuration files
that will be used in the next lesson to

--- Static IP Address and DNS ---

In this lesson we are going to further refine
our setup by associating a static IP address
and then registering that with a domain
record for easy access in the future.
For this lesson, in order to follow along exactly,
I recommend having access to a domain name you can manage
so that you can add a record to refer
to an IP address we will set up soon.
If you don't have access to a domain name you
can still watch and get an idea of what to do
or to recommend to your network administrator.
Currently I'm on the Amazon Lightsail
page, on the instances tab.
We have the instance we just created.
Associated with that instance is
a publicly accessible IP address.
Newly created instances are given one dynamically.
However, if we needed to stop our instance or replace it
with another copy we would be allocated another IP address.
While this might not sound like a big deal, most people don't
like having IP addresses to memorize for services they use,
especially if they happen to change sometimes.
That's why we have domain names.
The domain name system allows us to associate a user friendly
name, like "jenkins.example.com", to a specific IP address.
However, since domain records take several
hours or days to update around the world
it's not a good idea to use dynamically
allocated ones for this purpose.
That's where static ip addresses come in.
With AWS Lightsail you are allowed to
use up to 5 static IP addresses for free.
That should be more than enough for us;
we only need to associate a static IP
address for services endpoints or web sites
we need to share publicly or with
others on our team or company.
Let's start off by going over to the networking tab.
Then click on "Create static IP".
Once on the create a static IP address page you can change
the location where the IP address will be available,
but the default should be fine.
The main thing is the dropdown for selecting
which instance to attach the IP address.
Once the static IP address is associated with the specific
instance that instance's current IP address is released
and the static IP address becomes the publicly
accessible IP address for that particular instance.
That's why I like to setup a static
IP address as early on as possible.
Now click on the dropdown and select
the one and only instance we have.
Once you do that the dropdown will be
replaced with the instance name and details.
Like all our Lightsail resources we need
to give the static IP address a name.
Of course this would make more sense if we had multiple IP
addresses to manage, but either way it's still required.
Let's name the IP address after our instance,
like this: "Jenkins-Master-1-StaticIp".
While we're here there's one other quirk to
how static ip addresses work in Lightsail;
they must always be attached to an instance.
Otherwise you'll be charged a small fee
for every hour you have the static IP address
reserved but not otherwise attached anywhere.
However, the first hour is free so you can
change where the static IP address points.
Great. Now click on the create button
to create your static IP address.
Now that we have our very own static IP address,
copy that IP address to your clipboard
since we're going to be using it very soon.
Let's click back to home,
which brings us back to the instances tab.
Here you should note that the public IP address is
now the static IP address that was allocated for us.
This next part is optional but highly recommended.
You'll need access to a domain name you can manage.
Now that we have our own static IP address
I want to associate it with a domain record to
create a user friendly name for our Jenkins server.
While you can move your domain management
to Lightsail, you don't have to;
I'm just going to use my existing domain
registrar's domain management services.
Of course your domain registrar's interface will be
different, but the overall concepts should be about the same.
I'm going to open up another web browser tab,
and go to my domain registrar, and log into my account.
Once I'm logged in I will need to
navigate to the domain I want to update.
In this case I own a domain called "jenkins.training"
Within that page I need to navigate to the
DNS management interface for that domain.
Since Jenkins is a build server I want to create
a subdomain record called "build.Jenkins.training"
that will point to the static IP
address we created in Lightsail.
There are many different types of DNS records,
but the one we need to use is the "A Record".
That will allow us to associate a name to an IP address.
Once I select the "A Record" type
I will specify "build" as the name.
I only need to specify the subdomain part of the name.
Then I need to paste in the IP address from the clipboard.
I'll leave the time to live field as
automatic, and now I will save the record.
If you want you can bounce back to Lightsail to
make sure that we have the correct IP address.
Since the domain name system is a global network
it might take a couple of days before
this change is seen throughout the world.
Because of that the next thing we need to do is hack
our local system to see this association regardless.
On most systems there is a special file that
overrides any values in the Domain Name System.
This is the "hosts" file.
The networking stack always checks this file first before
resolving any given name using the domain name system.
While editing the host file is not
a recommended practice in general.
It does come in handy for situations just like this.
Also, if you actually don't have
access to a domain name you can manage.
this might be the only way you can continue to follow along.
On Mac and Linux the host file is located at "/etc/hosts".
Since this is a system level file we
need to elevate our access using sudo,
or log in as the root user, in order to edit this file.
I'm going to use a simple text editor, nano,
to edit the host file: "sudo nano /etc/hosts".
Of course you'll need to be logged in as
a user with sudo access for this to work.
Normally you will be prompted for your account
password when attempting to run anything with sudo.
After providing my password nano should be launched
with the contents of our existing host file loaded.
The format is fairly straightforward.
Each line represents a DNS entry.
The line starts with the IP address followed by the
domain name, which is separated by some whitespace.
Paste in the static IP address and then
type the full name to the Jenkins server.
I'm using the full name of "build.jenkins.training",
but of course you need to use your own domain name.
Once done making your changes press
"control+X" to begin the exit process.
Nano will prompt you if you want to
save the changes. Press Y for yes.
Then you'll be prompted for the file name; just press
enter to continue to use the existing file name.
On a Windows system it can be a bit more tricky.
Like on Mac and Linux you need to edit the
host file with the administrator rights.
Since I have Atom installed I can use the desktop
icon to run Atom as administrator, like so.
You might be prompted like this, just click yes to continue.
Now use your editor's file menu to open a file,
which should bring up an open file dialog like this.
I've already hunted down this file in a previous
session so Atom happens to remember this.
The host file on Windows is located in a special
folder located within your "windows" folder,
then "system32/drivers", and then finally "etc".
There should be a file called "hosts" located there.
Select that file to open it.
Let's do the same thing we did on the Mac and Linux side.
It follows the same format: IP address,
some whitespace, and then full domain name.
Once you're done making the changes
save the file and close your editor.
Great, now that that's done, let's head back over to Lightsail.
In this lesson we set up a static IP
address and associated it with a domain name
for a user friendly name for our Jenkins server.
However, since the DNS system takes
a while to propagate around the world
we also hacked our local system's host file to override
the domain resolution until the DNS system can catch up.
With all that in place we can move forward
with accessing our instance via SSH.

--- Instance Access ---

Welcome, in this lesson, we will access
our instance using SSH or secure shell.
In a previous lesson we associated our newly
created instance with a static IP address.
We can now use that IP address to login using the secure
shell program on the terminal or in Git bash on Windows.
Before we do that let's make a note of the IP address,
which should still be in our system's clipboard,
and the default username associated with the running instance.
This will be the username we will need to log in using SSH.
Now open your terminal program on your
system and type the following command:
"ssh ubuntu"; the "@" symbol; and then paste in the
static IP address; space; and then the "-I" option,
which allows us to specify the SSH key to use; space;
and finally the full path to the private
SSH key we generated in a previous lesson.
Your command should look something like this,
but of course using your specific IP address.
Now press enter to attempt to login.
Since this is our first attempt at connecting to this server we
will be prompted to accept the SSH fingerprint for this server.
Type yes and press enter to continue.
You should end up on a screen like this,
showing us some welcome text and some
details about potentially pending updates.
As mentioned before, one of the disadvantages is that
the available options are often somewhat outdated.
We will fix that a bit later on.
Right now, while we are here,
I want to check out the output of our launch script.
To do that I need to login as the root
user using the “sudo” command like this:
"sudo su -l" and then press enter.
"whoami"; you can see that we are
logged in as the root user now.
Most system logs are located within the "var/log" directory,
and that's where AWS puts the output log for the script.
Under the hood AWS uses the cloud-init
system to run our launch script,
and correspondingly there is a log
file named "cloud-init-output.log".
Which, as the name implies, has the output of any
commands that we specified during the launch script.
We can use the cat command to display the contents
of the file directly into the terminal, like this:
"cat cloud-init-output.log", then press enter.
Now let's scroll back up to some familiar commands.
Well I can see, at this point,
the output of the Java 8 installation process,
which is then followed by the Maven install,
then Jenkins itself,
and finally Nginx.
Towards the end we even see the call to GitHub
to download the Nginx configuration file.
Now that we've done that, let's exit out of the root session
and then exit out of the main session,
which will return us back to the terminal on our local system.
Now that we have successfully connected to
our instance using the static IP address,
let's try the same thing but using the domain name instead.
I'm going to use the up arrow to
bring back the previous command,
so I can just edit that instead of re-typing the entire thing.
Now just replace the IP address
with the domain name, like this.
Press enter to connect.
Like before we should be prompted to confirm the fingerprint.
That's all I wanted to show, so let's exit out of the session.
Once back to our terminal on our local
system I want to create an SSH configuration
that automatically uses the private key for our Jenkins server.
To do that go into your ".ssh" folder with your home directory.
Once there use a text editor to create or open a config file.
I'll use nano, like this: "nano config", then press enter.
Alright, I need to jump over to another terminal session
to remind myself of the full name to the private key.
I'll copy that to our clipboard for now.
Once back over to Nano, we can start with a host directive
and the name of the host for this configuration, like this.
Of course specify your own Jenkins server domain name.
On the next line, and indented a bit,
use the "User" directive and the name "Ubuntu".
Finally, on the last line,
also indented, use the "IdentityFile"
directive, and the full path
to the private key file.
You should now have an SSH config
file that looks something like this.
With all the changes done save and close the file.
On Nano that would be "control+X", then "Y", and enter.
Let's try out the SSH configuration now.
Use the up arrow key to display the previous command,
and then remove the "-I" option and the key file path.
This should be a much shorter command.
Press enter to confirm everything works.
Once you have confirmed that you can login to the instance
like before exit out to return to your local system.
In this lesson we logged into our instance using SSH,
then we refined things a bit by configuring
SSH to automatically use our private key
when connecting to our instance using the full domain name.
At this point we still haven't attempted
to access Jenkins via the web browser;
we will do that in the next lesson.

--- Restart within Lightsail ---

Welcome, in this lesson, we are going to stop, start,
and restart our Jenkins instance on AWS Lightsail.
While this might seem like a mundane thing to do,
stopping, starting, and restarting our instance
is something that is part of validating the
overall robustness of the Jenkins instance
and stopping the instance is actually needed in some cases.
Unlike normal AWS resources stopping a Lightsail
instance doesn't actually save you any money.
We will be warned about this when
we actually stop the instance.
If you need to save money, while you don't need the
instance, you need to delete the instance.
Of course that has its own issues, like if you have
a static IP address associated with the instance,
then you will be charged for the static
IP address while it remains unattached.
Just stopping an instance keeps the
static IP address associated with it.
However, if we did not have a static
IP address attached to the instance
then we would lose the dynamically allocated IP
address, since it would be released back to AWS.
Sometimes you just need to stop for instance.
One common reason for doing this is a complete
backup using the snapshotting feature.
We will cover that in the next lesson.
For now we just need to make sure that all our
services can weather the stop and start cycle.
I'm going to head over to my Jenkins server.
Right now I'm on the login page,

--- Instance Snapshots ---

Welcome, in this lesson, we are going to use

Before we create our first snapshot,

It's not expensive either, and AWS will optimize for costs
by only charging you the differences between the snapshots.
For example the first snapshot will take
the full 20 gigabytes of the drive space,
but if we do another snapshot tomorrow
and only two gigabytes changed
we will only get charged for the two
additional gigabytes of difference.
We won't be keeping the snapshot long enough
to incur much cost, so follow along if you can.
We can name our snapshots; you might
want to give a meaningful name.
if you are backing up before a
major update or significant changes.
However they don't matter that much if
snapshots are done on a regular basis.

cleanest possible backup, so I will stop the instance first.

instance to be fully stopped before proceeding

below with the date and time it was created.
We can expand out the details,
which shows us the 20 gigabytes on the system disk.
The three vertical dots to the side provides us some options,
really just to create an instance and to delete the snapshot.

--- Instance Flip ---

Welcome, in this lesson, we are going to create a new Jenkins
server instance based on the snapshot we just created.
Then we will perform a system upgrade
and, provided that was successful,
we will change over to the new Jenkins server.
All this is a common production rollout
pattern called zero downtime deployment.
In a previous lesson we made a backup of our
current Jenkins server by creating a snapshot.
A really useful thing we can do with snapshots is to
create an entirely new instance based on that snapshot.
This opens up some interesting possibilities.
We could use this ability to restore from
a known good previous instance state.
We could also try out changes without
impacting the active instance,
basically creating a sandbox or impromptu development server.
We could also apply the changes to a new copy of the instance,
once we are happy with it flip over to
make the new instance the active one.
This pattern is known as zero downtime deployment,
where we test in parallel our new production server
and simply flip over from the old server to the new
server quickly so it appears transparent to the end users.
From everyone else's perspective the deployment
or update incurred very little to no downtime.
That's what I'll show here.
To be fair, on a really active system
you'll want to develop a strategy
for dealing with any changes that happen on the active
server while performing the update on the new system.
From the snapshots tab on the instance page click on the
vertical three dots next to our snapshot we just created.
One option is to "Create new instance", click on that option.
That takes us to the "Create an instance from a snapshot" page.
This is a variation of the "Create
an instance" page we used earlier.
I really don't want to change any details right now;
I want to perform an operating system upgrade,
which is something that can't be scripted.
Leave all the defaults.
The instance should select the key pair we created earlier.
I want to use the same hosting plan too.
We just need to change the instance
name; I'll use Jenkins-Master-2.
Great, now click on create to create
our new instance based on the snapshot.
