--- Update HostKey ---

Welcome, in this lesson, we will update our
host key fingerprint for the new Jenkins master
so we can continue to log in to the instance via SSH.
We need to update our SSH Host key fingerprint
while connecting to our new Jenkins server.
Every time we replace the underlying server
instance the host key fingerprint will change
and since SSH cares about that part
of that keeping you secure thingy
we need to tell our local SSH
configuration about the new fingerprints.
Return to your terminal, or Git bash on Windows,
and attempt to connect to the Jenkins
server using the domain name we have setup
"ssh ubuntu@", and then the Jenkins server domain name.
When I attempt to connect the connection is
refused because SSH detects the wrong fingerprint
for the server it’s trying to connect with.
This check, or verification,
is an important part of the
connection process and helps prevent
the man-in-the-middle attack.
Basically if SSH doesn't trust the fingerprint
signature of the target's system then SSH assumes
there is a problem or a hack attempt and aborts the connection.
This is a good thing.
First, let's remove the old fingerprint
by using the following command:
"ssh-keygen -R", and then your Jenkins server domain name.
The "ssh-keygen" program should be able to find
the entry and remove it from our known hosts file.
Of course, you could also remove that
entry manually if you really wanted.
Now let's re-attempt: "ssh ubuntu@"
the Jenkins server domain name.
Since our known hosts file no longer has that entry
we will be prompted to confirm if we want to add
the new server fingerprint to our SSH configuration.
Of course type yes to continue.
Now, this time, we are successful and the
SSH host key has been updated in the process.
Since we're done for now exit out of the SSH
session with our Jenkins master instance,
which returns us back to our local system in terminal.
In this lesson we updated the host key fingerprint needed
to connect via SSH to our new Jenkins master instance.
In the next lesson we will get legit by enabling SSL
so we can be more secure while working within Jenkins.

--- Web Secured, Part 1 ---

Welcome, in this lesson, we're going to begin the process to
encrypt our web traffic to our Jenkins server by enabling SSL.
So far we have been accessing our Jenkins
server by using the standard HTTPS port of 80.
Even to go that far we had to use Nginx
to hide the Jenkins default port of 8080.
Because of that every time we logged into
Jenkins we have done so in the clear,
that is anyone sniffing traffic between us and our
server anywhere online could see the username password
and any other sensitive information.
Of course, this is bad.
When Jenkins was on our local system we
really didn't have to care about this,
nor is this a huge issue within the
confines of a corporate network,
provided your company is small enough
that you trust everyone that works there.
These days that's not a safe bet, especially if you
hire outside consultants or temporary employees.
But any server exposed to the open internet needs to be
locked down, including securing all traffic through it.
Great, so we need to secure the web traffic.
What are our options?
Well we could use the AWS Lightsail
approach, which I considered.
With Lightsail you can use a load balancer to attach a
certificate that would be used to encrypt the traffic.
This is the only supported way to
encrypt traffic to your instance.
You can't attach certificates
directly to the instance, and
there's no way to associate that
to the static IP address either.
The load balancer is the only way, which is not a huge deal.
With Jenkins not only do we not need the load balancer
for its original designed purpose of balancing traffic
between multiple copies of an instance but
Jenkins is actually unable to work in that way,
since the Jenkins configuration, build information, etc.
are all stored on the Jenkins server, making it stateful.
Let's not go into that too much right now;
basically Jenkins is just not well suited for high
availability configuration, like load balancers.
However we could still use the load balancer
and only have one instance serviced by it,
then we could attach the Amazon managed certificate
to the load balancer thus securing Jenkins.
While all certificates managed by AWS are
free, the load balancer is not.
And, oddly enough, it's not cheap either, at least to me.
As of the recording of this course the load
balancer costs eighteen dollars per month.
Considering that's several times the
cost of the instance we're using,
and we're not even using the load
balancing aspect of the functionality.
If I could use the load balancing and I had a small army
of Jenkins servers behind it it wouldn't be a big deal.
The SSL certificate would be a total bonus.
However, since I can only use the load
balancer to host the SSL certificate,
then effectively I would be paying 18 dollars per month,
or over two hundred dollars a year, for the certificate.
I can get an SSL certificate for way less, in fact free.
We'll get to that in a moment.
Since I'm not using an Amazon supplied method of associating a
certificate to the instance we'll need to use Nginx for that.
That's actually another reason for
having Nginx in front of Jenkins;
It's a lot easier to add HTTPS support to Nginx than Jenkins.
Before we jump into securing the Jenkins server,
I want to use the production flip approach we
did earlier when we updated the operating system.
This will allow us to quickly recover if something goes wrong.
We will start by creating a snapshot of our Jenkins server
instance, following the same basic steps we had before.
Drill into the instance page for Jenkins-Master-2,
go to the snapshots tab and create the snapshot.
While we wait for our snapshot to complete let's learn
about SSL certificates, since this could take a while.
In order to secure website traffic you need to enable HTTPS,
which is the secure version of the HTTP protocol
that runs on port 443 instead of port 80.
HTTPS requires a valid certificate that has been issued by one
of the certificate authorities respected by your web browser.
In fact, most companies selling certificates are really just
re-selling them from a supported Certificate Authority or CA..
There's really nothing shady about it, most companies
will tell you which CA a certificate is associated.
Certificates can have many attributes,
the most important one is the type of certificate.
Right now the most secure is TLS.
After that how the certificate was validated is also a
consideration, although a distant second in my opinion.
In the certificate issuing world there are three
flavors of certificates: domain verified or DV,
organization verified or OV, and extended verified or EV.
Domain verified are easier to get and can be automated,
since the requester only has to prove they
own or control the domain being secured
Since the certificate issued can be automated they are
generally a lot less expensive, and in some case even free.
Some certificate issuing
companies may claim these domain
validated certificates are somehow less
secure, but they aren't.
They use the same encryption
technology as any other certificate.
The main difference is that organization
verified certificates feel more secure
because the certificate issuing company has validated the
business or company seems legit before issuing certificate,
or at least that's what their marketing material says.
Very few certificate issuing companies will refuse to sell
you a domain validated certificate if that's what you want.
Just saying, if they weren't any good no legitimate company
would sell them and all major web browsers would reject them.
Of course, this isn't the case.
Organization verified certificates can only validate
that a business or organization is legally set up.
Of course, there are plenty of bad
companies that are legally set up.
Because of that some certificate issuing companies have an
additional offering they call extended verify certificates,
where they promise to do some research to make sure that a
business is in good standing before issuing the certificate.
The bottom line is there is no technical
difference between the certificates,
the only difference is around the
perceived trust and nothing more.
In fact the vast majority of users
don't even check the certificate.
They might look to see if the golden padlock
is in place or not, but even that much is rare.
I've never seen a web browser warn its users
that certificate is only domain verified.
I highly recommend any web site or service
be secured by any type of certificate,
especially if you are passing sensitive
information around, like logging credentials.
There is a project called Let's Encrypt that
offers domain validated SSL certificates for free.
There are a few things to know. First these
certificates can only be issued for 90 days at a time,
and they encourage renewal every 60 days.
The project says this is to encourage automation
while still providing a long enough window
for those that need to manually renew.
They also claim it's a shorter window if
something bad happens to the certificate.
As mentioned before the certificates issued our
domain validated, so if your security engineer
doesn't share the same high opinion of them as I
do then you might have a political battle to fight.
However, since this process can be automated and it's free
it can be done now instead of waiting days
or weeks for any other type of certificate.
When we created a duplicate Jenkins server to illustrate how
to flip using a static IP address to point to a new instance
we could have provided another launch script, but we didn't.
In this case we need to.
I have a script that will automate the making
of a few updates to our Nginx configuration
to get ready for the Let's Encrypt process.
We will still need to do a few manual steps
afterwards, but this will get us most of the way there.
Let's return to the Jenkins Bootcamp course
repository that we have used previously.
Once there go into the Lightsail folder.
Click into the "jenkins-secure.sh" file.
That will be the launch script for the new instance.
Once the file opens it starts like all bash
scripts, with the leading shebang.
After that I want to stop the Nginx service,
since we will be changing its configuration.
Then create a Jenkins directory for serving static content,
which will be required by the Let's Encrypt process.
After that, if the previous configuration is present delete it.
We will replace it with a new configuration
file, which we will cover soon.
That configuration file is downloaded from GitHub
and placed in the available sites folder for Nginx.
Then a symbolic link is established between the new
configuration file and the "sites-enabled" folder,
which is how Nginx knows which configuration to load or not.
After verifying the configuration for Nginx is
acceptable, I will start the Nginx service again.
With the updates to Nginx out of the way, the rest of the
script updates our system with a new program called certbot
that helps us automate the creation and retrieval
of certificates from the Let's Encrypt service.
At the very end I provide a commented outline of the command
we will need to use to generate our first certificate.
Of course, when we actually run the command
you will need to provide your own domain name.
Now, let's back up a bit and look
at the "jenkins-secured.conf" file.
That will be our new Nginx configuration file.
This file is much more involved than our previous configuration
file, although the top is similar to what we have now,
except I added a few new tweaks, like log file location
and an alias for anything in a ".well-known" folder,
that's the part needed for Let's Encrypt.
Beyond that it should be about the same.
The biggest difference is in the additional server block.
The top block only listens for port 80, the bottom
server block listens only on 443, and has SSL turned on.
I specify the server name directive since
that is required by the certificate.
I also have several SSL oriented settings.
Some are commented out until we run certbot to fetch the
cert, and the other settings are benign.
Okay, by now the snapshot should be complete.
From the snapshots tab let's create a new instance
much in the same way we did in a previous lesson.
Click on the vertical dots, and select "Create new instance".
Once on the "Create an instance from a snapshot" page
hop back over to our GitHub
repository, go into the
"secure-jenkins.sh" file and copy
its contents to the clipboard.
Then return to Lightsail, expand out the launch script link
into a text box for our code. Paste in the script contents.
Everything else should be the same, except I would
like to provide the name. I'll use "Jenkins-Master-3".
Great. Now click "Create" to create our new instance.
That'll take us back to the instances page, where we can
see our new instance progressing from pending to running
Normally I would like to wait until everything
is completely updated on a new server
before flipping the static IP address over.
However, the domain verification process
used by Let's Encrypt needs to see
that I'm serving content from the
domain I'm attempting to secure.
Once our new instance is running click on the networking tab.
Go into the static IP address configuration.
Now detach from the current instance, select the new
instance, and attach the static IP address there.
At this point our domain name will
be pointing to the new instance.
Now we need to allow for a HTTPS traffic to the new instance.
Return to the instances tab and then go into
the details for the Jenkins-Master-3 instance.
Go to the networking tab there and
add a new rule for the firewall.
Select HTTPS, which should fill
in the other values. Click save.
Now we can accept HTTPS traffic to our instance.
In this lesson we started adding the SSL and a
HTTPS support to our Jenkins server via Nginx
using an awesome free service called Let's Encrypt
In the next lesson we will finish that process and test it out.

--- Web Secured, Part 2 ---

Welcome, in this lesson, we're going to finish the process to
encrypt our web traffic to our Jenkins server by enabling SSL.
We need to login to our new instance via SSH.
Let's remove any previous known host entries using
"ssh-keygen -R", then the domain name for Jenkins.
Once done we can now connect using SSH using the
Jenkins domain name like we've done in previous lessons.
Of course, we will be prompted to accept the new fingerprint.
Once logged in I need to assume root: "sudo su -l".
Now let's check on the Jenkins folder for
Nginx; I just want to make sure it got created.
Nginx is configured to look for web content in "/var/www":
"cd /var/www".
Now let's look at the files and folders here, "ll".
Great, looks like that Jenkins folder was created.
Now let's move to the Nginx sites available
configuration folder: "cd /etc/nginx/sites-available".

--- Beyond Lightsail ---

Welcome, in this lesson, we're going to discuss ways
to grow within Lightsail and perhaps even beyond.
So far, we have been using Lightsail like a
virtual private server or VPS hosting provider
just built on top of the AWS resources.
And, for the most part, that's exactly what it is.
Using Lightsail provides us an easy onboarding
into the world of Amazon Web Services or AWS.
Since Lightsail is implemented using AWS resources
you get gracefully introduced to AWS concepts.
At no point are you required to know how stuff works, but you
can often customize instances or change where they're deployed.
After that, without having to deal with the
complexity that normally comes with all that is AWS.
Also, AWS seems to be improving
Lightsail by introducing new services.
For example Amazon recently introduced database services
all at the same level of simplicity and
constant pricing as the rest of Lightsail.
However Lightsail is somewhat limited; you
often give up power an option for simplicity.
If Lightsail fulfills your needs,
that's a tradeoff worth considering.
Even so, you can still start in Lightsail and then
connect to standard AWS resources using VPC peering.
That feature allows a special link between
Lightsail and the default VPC in your AWS account.
VPC stands for virtual private cloud and it's really just
a way to group related to the AWS resources together.
While your account can have multiple VPCs all AWS accounts
recently set up come with at least one default VPC.
Lightsail really is another VPC, but all those details are
hidden from you since AWS fully manages all those resources.
The main point is that you can configure Lightsail to
link to your default VPC in the standard AWS account
so you can use other AWS resources like other instances,
Docker orchestration, or database managed outside Lightsail.
Even remaining inside Lightsail
there are still additional steps we might want to do before
we would consider our Jenkins master ready for production use.
First, I noticed that I occasionally run into an Nginx
proxy error with a 502 error page or even a 504 error page.
While it initially looked like the smallest
Lightsail instance was going to work,
it actually is just a bit too small for any real world use.
You'll need to consider enlarging
the Jenkins server and possibly
using build nodes to distribute
the workload of any builds.
I'll cover that in the next section.
Next, while Amazon does a pretty good job of
providing a secure environment to setup our server
there are still hardening steps that we
need to do at the operating system level.
There are a handful of automation tasks that
should be set up for long term use on the instance.
For example you need to set up a cron job to automatically
renew the Let's Encrypt SSL certificate every 60 days.
You could also look at automatic nightly
or at least weekly snapshot backups.
With those considerations taken into account
I think this would be a pretty good starting
point for a production ready Jenkins build server.
At some point you might outgrow what Lightsail can offer.
As discussed, you can use VPC peering
to leverage standard AWS resources
but you might decide to keep
everything together in one spot in AWS.
At that point you could still use the concepts we
have learned about in Lightsail and apply them in AWS.
We can even use the launch scripts we developed
as starting points for new EC2 instances in AWS,
or as the basis for a configuration as code tool like
Chef, Puppet, Ansible, or SaltStack.
You can think of infrastructure in the
cloud as an iterative development workflow.
We start out manually setting things
up to see what works and what doesn't,
then we use simple scripts to automate instance setup.
Ultimately you'll want to research something like Cloud
Formation or Terraform to represent infrastructure as code,
even bringing it back into Jenkins.
We could literally build Jenkins with Jenkins.
In this lesson we reviewed what's possible within
Amazon Lightsail and how to grow beyond it.

--- Scaling Jenkins in AWS Lightsail Overview ---

Welcome, in this section we will start off
with some basic concepts around scaling,
which for the most part is the rest of this lesson.
Then we will look at scaling up by moving to a larger instance,
and then testing out the results.
Finally the rest of this section is dedicated to scaling out
by adding more instances to provide a distributed build system.
At some point you will reach the limit of what you can do on your Jenkins server.
In fact, preparing for this course I realized
the free tier instance wasn't going to work very well.
I'll show you what I encountered in an upcoming lesson.
But, the point is I found myself needing to scale Jenkins almost immediately.
These limitations are fairly common issues
most web applications of all kind have to face eventually,
that is if they're successful or well used.
If we don't scale our infrastructure, we will experience unacceptable performance
perhaps only during peak usage times.
At some point Jenkins might even become unresponsive and start crashing.
So, what are the options?
Like most applications you can either scale up
by replacing the existing hardware with larger faster or better systems,
or you can scale out by adding more hardware and distributing the load across multiple systems.
And even a combination of both approaches: up and out.
Probably the easiest to understand is scaling up;
just buy bigger hardware, which could be physical or virtual.
Since we have started using AWS this means larger instances.
Of course, there are limits to this approach.
First, you have to consider the Jenkins server,
any web proxies on top of it and any resources needed
to efficiently execute any builds.
The more concurrent builds you expect the larger the instance required,
which is an increased cost, perhaps by a significant amount.
Another approach to scaling is by adding more hardware to the problem,
or, in our case, more instances.
In doing so we will separate out the web proxy, Jenkins master,
and any processes needed to execute builds on their own instances.
Currently Jenkins doesn't really support the idea of having multiple masters load balanced
since the file system is the Jenkins database,
however Jenkins does support the idea of build agents or slave systems.
This is commonly known as the master-slave architecture.
The Jenkins master can have one or many instances
or systems simply available to run build jobs.
Jenkins uses SSH or another connection protocol
to utilize computer power on slave machine instances.
When Jenkins first establishes a connection with a slave
Jenkins sends over an agent program,
which is used by Jenkins for managing the builds and querying any resource availability.
Like several things in Jenkins, there are multiple terms associated with the master slave relationship;
Jenkins sometimes refers to these slave systems as build nodes.
The Jenkins slave or build node is a physical or virtual hardware
and the Jenkins agent is a little program that runs on the slave
to help orchestrate the builds upon the slave or build node
If needed you could also use a combination of scaling up and out by using more larger instances.
At some point you might reach the limits of both scaling up and scaling out
Then what?
That's where multi-master build farms come into play.
There is nothing to say that you must run everything on a single Jenkins master.
Even with the biggest, fastest hardware eventually you might need more.
You can always accomplish this by either dividing up Jenkins masters
by functionality, that is builds, tests, and deploys
or by team, company division, or application group, or segmentation.
One major benefit of doing this is that changes on one Jenkins master
won't affect other teams or projects.
But that approach also has the most ongoing support cost,
since you have to increase the number of Jenkins masters, slaves, and other resources
that go along into the greater build platform.
So far I have attempted to keep costs down, perhaps even free, where possible.
Well, it's hard to scale if you aren't willing to spend any money.
So if you aren't able or willing to spend some money trying out AWS
then just sit back and watch the lessons and take notes.
Also, depending on how your AWS account is
you might need to ask AWS is for increased resource limits.
Let's take a moment and see how to request an increase to the limits.
I'm starting off in my Amazon light sail account,
and I'm currently walking through creating more instances for an upcoming lesson
while I am creating a new instance I encountered an error because I already have two instances.
While that seems low, I double checked the documentation on AWS resource limits.
All accounts have limits to prevent out of control resource allocation and crazy high bills as a result,
but two instances seems a bit low.
According to the documentation accounts should start off with 20 instances per region.
That's also the normal EC2 instance limit as well,
so it seems logical they would be the same.
It turns out that based on several factors, like account age and number of successful billing cycles
your actual limits may be less than what's documented.
Unfortunately, this fact, and the various factors, aren't well documented
so keep that in mind if you just signed up for an account .
Anyway, to request the limit increase go to the account dropdown
and then select "customer support".
Once there, click on "Create case" to open a new support case to Amazon.
This is such a common request, they have a dedicated option for it.
Select "Service limit increase" in the regarding field.
We need to select lightsail from the limit type dropdown
Then, within the request 1 box, select your region you're planning to work in.
I'm using "US East (Northern Virginia)".
Next choose the limit, which is actually the resource that needs to be increased.
I need more instances.
Once the limit resource has been selected the "New limit value" field should appear.
Provide the value you think you'll need.
The documented account limit is fine; I will specify 20
If there are other items you need to increase, you can add other requests,
however I'm content with asking for more instances.
Now, in the "Use Case Description" provide a justification for the increase.
I'm just gonna point out that the documentation specifies 20 instances.
Later on I will add another comment about needing this for a technical course
using their services as a cloud platform.
I'll leave the support language as English, since that's what I speak and contact method will be web.
I could have them call me, but I screen my calls these days,
which means I might miss a call from an unfamiliar number.
Once done filling out the form, click submit.
That brings us to the case details page,
which basically confirms we created a new case to the support team.
The resolution or response time will be based on the AWS support plan.
Since I decided to go with the basic plan, which is included with all AWS accounts,
that gives me the lowest priority, especially since I submitted this on a weekend.
I'll keep an eye on my email for any Amazon support responses.
Now it's just a waiting game.
OK, now it's been a few days since I submitted the request to increase the account limits,
and I finally have a response.
W en I return to the support page under account and then customer support

Clicking on the case, I can see the details regarding the resolution.
Basically, if we review the start of the case until Amazon agreed to increase my limit,
it was about two or three days.
Then it took me a few days to verify the limits were indeed increased,
and to close my case in response.
I point this out, because you need to plan accordingly.
Part of the time was waiting for an Amazon support person to respond to my case ticket,
which, since it was opened on the weekend,
it wasn't until early Monday morning following that weekend.
Then, it took about a day or so of back and forth discussions until the limits were increased for me.
In this lesson I introduced you to the scaling section by going over why you might need the scale Jenkins
and the options to consider, specifically scaling up and scaling out.
We ended with me submitting a case to increase my account limits,
and the follow up when the request was granted.

--- Bigger is Better ---

Welcome.
In this lesson, we will take things to the next level by scaling Jenkins up, by moving to a much larger
instance.
As you may have experienced already, the Jenkins server occasionally experiences issues.
These issues can be temporarily resolved by restarting Jenkins.
But this isn't a long term solution.
While testing out more involved builds on the Jenkins server, I quickly found the limitation of the
instance type I selected for Jenkins.
While it qualifies for a free month, it really isn't large enough to run Nginx Jenkins and any complex
builds at the same time.
I kept running into proxy server issues from Nginx reporting 502 and 504 errors.
For me, this is all good.
This gives me a great example of when we need to consider moving to a more powerful system.
For Jenkins.
In reality, since Jenkins is a web application that runs on Java, it really needs a certain amount
of memory to run.
And if you plan on running a Java build within it, say a maven or ant build that requires even more
processor power and memory to get the job done.
This became clear when Jenkins would appear to be fine with simple shell commands, but completely stopped
working when running an example Maven project, Jenkins requires at least 512MB of Ram and two gigabytes
of disk space.
While our instance is perfectly fine with 20GB of disk space, the 512MB for the entire system just
was below what we needed for Jenkins to run for any long periods of time.
Remember that 512MB is used by many more processes besides Jenkins.
So Jenkins really has less than 512MB on our Lightsail instance, sometimes it doesn't pay to go cheap.
That's fine.
Since Jenkins lasted long enough to make it through the previous section, although you might have needed
to restart it a few times.
CloudBees The companies that oversees the development of Jenkins and several plugins recommends a good
starting point of four gigabytes of Ram with at least 50GB of disk space.

--- Bigger Project ---

Welcome in this video.

--- Scale Up Design ---

In this lesson, we're going to talk about the design or plans for scaling out our Jenkins server infrastructure.
While it's fun to jump into the actual implementation, it really pays to take a moment to think about
how to implement the scaling out process.
In particular, we need to plan out how many instances we will need, what sizes and for what purpose.
First, we covered scaling our Jenkins server instance up by adding more resources, disk, space,
memory and compute power.
However, scaling up only can take you so far.
At some point you need to look at scaling out that is, adding more instances into the mix.
To scale out, we're going to use four instances For this example, I want to start out by having a
dedicated Nginx web proxy server that will terminate our SSL connection for Https that is secure web
traffic and proxy it to the Jenkins master on Port 8080.
We'll need to update the firewall rules for this instance to allow https traffic and this will also
be the instance we attach the static IP address to serve for our domain name associated with our Jenkins
system.
Since Nginx is fairly lightweight, the smallest instance plan will be fine, which is great.
We can save a little on hosting fees here.
The Jenkins master will be on its own instance since Jenkins listens on Port 8080, we will replace
the standard Http firewall rule with a custom rule for port 8080.
The Jenkins master will really just be a front end for the projects and builds we manage using Jenkins.
The actual builds will occur on dedicated build nodes attached to the Jenkins master using SSH.
Those instances won't need Http traffic at all, so we'll update the firewall rules for those instances
accordingly.
In reality, we could add as many build node instances as we need to support our demand.
This is the primary way that scaling happens on a Jenkins system.
The next level up would be to replicate the entire Jenkins setup for each business unit, group or team.
The Jenkins master and any build nodes or slaves will have a mid-range $10 plan for each instance or
$30 total.
This provides us enough resources to get the job done, but isn't too expensive to run all the time.
If you run for the full month for the entire infrastructure, we're only talking about $34 a month,
assuming we stay within our data transfer allocation, which should be easy starting out, of course
this is greater than zero, but if your company already pays for AWS resources, this isn't a new concept
with the exception of the static IP address associated with the Nginx web proxy, all the communication
between our instances will be done using the private IP addresses.
This saves us on traffic costs.
If we use the public IP addresses, the traffic will count against our ingress and egress, traffic
allocation and possible overages.
Also, it's just a more secure practice to communicate on all private addresses where possible.
This infrastructure setup has a lot of potential.
We could scale up by replacing each instance with an even larger instance.
Still, we could add more build nodes to the mix.
Also allows us the flexibility to shut down.
That is, remove most of our Jenkins infrastructure by just leaving the web proxy running with the attached
static IP address.
This is a great option for proof of concept testing or development.
Jenkins servers, of course, for production servers, you want to run the servers all the time.
Even with this setup, we have the potential to use our VPC peering connection to leverage standard
AWS resources to scale even further with Docker orchestrated slaves using ECS or Eks.
In this lesson, we took a moment to think about what our scaled out Jenkins infrastructure should look
like before we just start randomly spinning up stuff.
Now that we have a plan.
In the next lesson, we will start with the first phase, which is implementing a secure web proxy using
Nginx.

In a previous lesson, this file was already downloaded into the instance.
We're going to review the code here before we execute it.
Starting at the top, after the standard shebang, the script checks the current user.
Since this script must be ran as the root user, then I check for the required command line arguments.
The first one is the domain name used with Jenkins.
This will be the domain name that we have been using with the static IP address.
The second argument is the email address that is associated with the certificate that will be generated
by the Letsencrypt service.

more options than we've used before.
We'll pass in options like agreeing to the terms of service so the command can be fully automated.
The Certbot program will use the Nginx web route as the context for the domain control challenge.
Assuming this step is successful, the new SSL TLS certificate will be issued and downloaded to our
system.
After getting our certificate, the script downloads an Nginx configuration file for the secured version
of our website setup, which is then enabled right after replacing a placeholder for the domain name,
we then validate our Nginx configuration is valid and restart Nginx for everything to take effect.
After the shell script, return to the scale folder and open the web dash secured dot conf file that
will be the secured version of the configuration file for nginx.
Since this configuration only listens on port 443, we can have this configuration file active alongside
the non secure version.
You'll also notice we have a domain underscore name for the server name directive and other places throughout
the file.
The shell script will replace all places with the actual domain name you pass in to the script.
When we run it that way, this configuration will be set up to work correctly with the domain name We
are working with, in addition to all the other standard stuff for an Nginx configuration file, there
are a handful of settings specifically for configuring the SSL aspects of the server.

--- Jenkins Global Tools ---

Welcome.
In this video, we're going to update our Jenkins configuration with the build tool locations for our
build nodes once they're attached.
I'm currently on the Jenkins dashboard.
Let's go to manage Jenkins, then global tool configuration.
This is where we will tell Jenkins about where to find the build tools our Jenkins master will use for
builds.
There are two primary ways to set up technologies programming languages, and build tools for Jenkins,
either dynamically or statically.
There are trade offs to each way.
Dynamically would mean that you would allow Jenkins to install the required tools upon demand.
The main cost is a little upfront time to install the tool if it isn't already available on the build
node being used.
There is also a slight risk that the installation could fail and thus fail the build.
Also, this requires there be a Jenkins plugin that knows how to download and install the required technology.
Your build requires and being associated with that Jenkins project.
An advantage to this approach would be that it would be easier to install new versions of those tools
as the corresponding Jenkins plugin adds support for it without reprovisioning the build nodes or recreating
them from scratch.
However, then it becomes harder to script the lockdown set of available tools in an infrastructure
as code sort of way.
Statically associated tools are build tools or technologies that are already pre-installed on the build
node being used.
Jenkins already doesn't need to know they exist per se.
As long as you can access them via the command line or in a shell script, however, we can tell Jenkins
about them with the plugins that support this and that can provide some advantages or additional build
options.
The Jenkins Global Tools provides for configuring either tool setup or both.
For this lesson, we need to log in to one of the build node instances so we can grab the paths to the
specific tools or technologies.
Take one of the build node public IP addresses and then log in via SSH.
I'm able to use my up arrow, which in my bash history happens to be one of the build node sessions
once logged in.

I found the JVM folder.

Looks like we have a folder and a symlink to that folder.
This is probably the actual JDK location we need to use.

--- Attach Build Nodes ---

Welcome.
In this lesson, we're going to attach our build node instances to our Jenkins master.
So far we have created an Nginx secured web proxy that serves https traffic to our Jenkins master.
We have recently created to build node instances and we have added the private key needed to authenticate
with those build nodes.
Now it's time to connect the Jenkins master to the build node instances.
I'm starting off on the Jenkins dashboard and now that we have the SSH credentials item set up, we
can now proceed to attaching our first build node or slave to the Jenkins master from the dashboard.
Go to manage Jenkins and then go to manage nodes.
Here is where we will create and manage our build nodes associated with Jenkins.
In fact, we already have one set up by default.
The Jenkins master itself is considered a build node.
However, we configured it to only be used when explicitly referenced by Jenkins job to add a new build
node, click on the new node menu item.
Once on that page, give the node a name.
I'll call this builder.
One Permanent agent is the only type of build node available right now.
Of course, like most things in Jenkins, other types of build nodes are possible via the right plugins,
but as it turns out, this is actually what we need now.
Click okay to proceed.
Once on the node configuration page, the name field is already provided carrying it forward from the
previous page.
Give this node a reasonable description.
The number of executors can be a bit of trial and error, but I recommend going beyond no more than
two times the number of CPUs or cores available in this case.
I know the build node instance only has one virtual CPU.
I'll set the executors to two.
Think of the executors as the number of concurrent threads the slave can handle at once.
Too many executors will overburden the slave system and actually the performance will be slower than
fewer executors and requiring the builds to queue up the remote root directory is the location where
to perform the builds on the slave system.
Typically I set this to the home directory.
We will use the log in to the slave system, which is slash home slash Jenkins.
I could use another location as long as the log in user has full permissions in that directory.
Labels are how Jenkins jobs can be associated with specific slaves.
While I advocate making generic build nodes, especially when they are physical or virtual machine based
instances, it is a good idea to give your build nodes reasonable labels.
You can think of labels as tags or short names for the build nodes.
I like making my labels based on the technologies found on those build nodes, but any reasonable system
will be fine.
Each individual label can be separated by a space.
For example, Maven Space Java.
Space Packer.
Space TerraForm.
Space Builder.
Of course, I installed a lot more stuff on the build nodes than these, but this will just get us started.
Also, I like including a more generic label.
In addition to the specific technologies, usage refers to how to use the slave or build node, they
can either be exclusive, which means that the Jenkins job must specify the matching label for the slave
in order to be used, or they can be non-exclusive which Jenkins will consider the slave available to
perform any build.
The launch method refers to the connection or communication method to establish a link between the Jenkins
master and the slave system.
SSH is what we need for this slave in the host field.
Put the private IP address of the slave instance.
The key thing is consistent.
Let's make sure we use the IP address for the Jenkins builder one here.
Hop back over to lightsail if needed.

--- Update Build Nodes ---

Welcome in this lesson.

We need to check the item environment variables which will expand out to allow us to add a name of a
variable and its corresponding value.
We just need to add one variable named nvm underscore dir which will point to the directory where NVM
is installed on our build node, which is slash user slash local slash nvm.
This is needed since Jenkins uses a non interactive shell to log in and connect to the slave instance
which skips the normal shell init scripts like the bash RC file.
When every build happens, this environment variable and its value will be made available to any shell
commands that need it.
Once the environment variable has been added, click save to save the changes to this build nodes configuration.
Of course, this change won't take effect immediately.
Instead, we need to stop or disconnect the build node and reconnect for the environment variable to
be available for the builds click disconnect from the side menu.
You'll be prompted to provide a reason for disconnecting the build node which you can put anything you
want here and then click yes to confirm taking the build node offline.
Once back on the build node status page, you can click the launch agent button to attempt reconnecting
again.
This takes us to a page with our build node connection log.

on this slave.

Here it is nvm underscore dir which points to slash user slash local slash nvm.

second build node.

--- Matrix Project ---

Welcome.
In this lesson, we will create and run a matrix job that will be used to run a build on both slaves
at once.
Our current Jenkins project, a simple freestyle job that runs a shell script, confirms we are able
to execute builds on a remote slave server.
However, the way this project works, it can only run on one slave or build node at a time.
And if we use generic labels or no labels at all, Jenkins will prefer the same build node over and
over again, provided there's availability.
A script like this one to verify each slave instance was set up correctly needs to run on all build
nodes and preferably at the same time.
As it turns out, there's a plugin for that and it's already available on our Jenkins instance.
If you need to jump back over to the script and make sure the contents are still in your system's clipboard.
Then from the Jenkins dashboard, go to new item.
I'm going to name this validate dash all dash build dash nodes.

This allows you to create jobs that will work across multiple configurations and build nodes at the
same time.
If we allow it.
Click okay to advance to the configure page under general check the item execute concurrent builds if
necessary.
This allows multiple builds to run at the same time.
For this type of project we have a special section called Configuration Matrix where we define all the
different ways we need to run this.
Build the axis is the way to configure those different ways.
I'm going to choose slaves.
Doing so provides us a list of slaves or build agents to specify by label or by name.
Expand out the individual nodes and then select both slaves Builder one and Builder two.

--- More Clean Up ---

Welcome in this lesson.
Since we are nearing the end of this section, I want to show how to tear down this type of Jenkins
infrastructure in AWS to minimize cost while not using it.
Of course, backing up what we might want to restore later on.
To be fair, if you are building Jenkins out for a production system, then you might just want to watch
this lesson instead of following along.
However, if you are building a proof of concept development or testing type of Jenkins setup and you
plan not to use this configuration for a while, then you can dramatically reduce your monthly cost
by shutting down what you can and restore later when you're ready to resume using it.
I'm starting out on the main Lightsail interface on the Instances tab.
We currently have three instances left the Nginx secured web proxy, the Jenkins Master and one remaining
Jenkins slave which I have called Jenkins Builder one For this approach.
I don't want to delete all my instances because I don't want to lose my static IP address that I have
set up with a domain name record that provides a nice user friendly name to the Jenkins server.
In this distributed setup, however, the static IP address actually points to the Nginx instance,
not directly to the Jenkins master.
Because of that, we can delete both the Jenkins slave and the master instance and leave the Nginx server
running as is.
Granted.
Any traffic that goes to it will be met with an error page, but that's okay.
We were planning on tearing down everything anyway.
Also, since the nginx instance is the least expensive instance plan, it won't cost that much to keep
it around since we get charged by the hour for unattached static IP addresses or we have to give them
up.
I think this is a fair price to pay considering I'll probably want to come back to this setup in a few
weeks or perhaps a month or two.
Of course, your situation will be different if you plan not to use these systems for shorter periods,
say a few days, then the cost savings might not be worth the effort anyway, since I'm not sure when
I'll resume working with this Jenkins infrastructure and I like showing all my work, I'll take you
along with this journey with me, including the restoring everything in the next lessons.
Let's start off by backing up the instance we plan to delete and for best results, we need to stop
each instance first.

While waiting, let's do the same thing for the Jenkins master.
Go into that instance.

snapshots tab at the lightsail top level.
While we wait, let's check on the storage pricing.
Lightsail has pretty good documentation on one of the frequently asked pages.
There is a question and answer regarding Lightsail snapshot costs.
Like most of AWS Lightsail snapshots are a consumption based pricing currently at $0.05 per one gigabyte
stored per month.
That means for both instances, since they are 60GB each and yes, we are charged for the full 60GB
of the image and not the amount used on the disk.
That is a total of 120GB of storage costs, which comes out to $6 per month for our backups.

our web browser provided we aren't working off a cached copy, Nginx should eventually respond with
an error page.
Great.
Let's return to the Lightsail main page.
At the peak of this section we had four instances, three at the $10 a month level and one at the three
and a half dollars level.
That's about $34 per month.
Round it up plus any taxes?
Honestly, that's not bad considering what we're getting and what the cost could have been if we were
hosting this on prem, especially if you had to purchase new hardware up front and possibly the labor
costs for anyone needed to assist with setting this up.
However, for some, even $34 a month is still a lot.
In an earlier lesson, we reduced our cost a bit by getting rid of one unneeded slave instance and then
updated our Jenkins configuration accordingly.
That saved us $10 a month at that point.
So now we're down to $24 a month.
Now both the Jenkins master and the remaining slave instances terminated.
We save an additional $20 a month.
But in order to do that, we had to incur a potential $6 a month in snapshot storage costs.
Since we are keeping the $4 a month Nginx instance around to hold on to that static IP address.
And the snapshots cost $6.
We're looking at about $10 a month total to keep things like it is.
There are two possible alternatives, but both have their issues.
First, we could tear down everything and recreate it when we're ready to continue.
While it took a while to get to the finished state, I was explaining each step in great detail, plus
any additional time needed for whatever reasons.
In reality, if you don't mind spending about 20 or 30 minutes to recreate everything from the scripts
I've provided with validation and configuration on Jenkins, it might be worth saving that $10 a month.
Granted, you would lose that static IP address and that could chew a couple of days for DNS to completely
catch up with a new static IP address.
In any case, that might be worth the trade off.
Second, if you have a very new account, you might consider moving over to the main AWS account.
Some of the resources might be covered by the free tier for the first 12 months of your account.
Of course, if you're using an account older than a year, that won't save you much.
Also, the larger instances are still not free, but the Nginx server could be as long as your account
remained eligible.
The major downside is the significant learning curve that comes with the rest of AWS.
But in the long run that might be where you're headed anyway.
In this lesson, we backed up the instances we're likely to restore in the future before deleting them
to save on costs.

in this lesson and making the required adjustments since they will be given new IP addresses and host

--- Resolve Problem with MacOS Upgrade Breaking Git (new!) ---

Welcome, in this video, I'm going to show the common
issue that happens with Git, as provided by Apple,
after the yearly Mac OS upgrade and how to fix it.
Every year Apple has provided a significant
update to Mac OS. This year is no exception.
However, each time that happens Git,
along with other utilities provided by Apple's command
line developer tools, get messed up in the update.
Now this doesn't happen with the
occasional patches throughout the year;
for example going from 10.13.3 to
10.13.4 won't cause any issues.
This seems to happen only during the more
significant code name changing updates
like upgrading from High Sierra (10.13) to Mojave (10.14)
Prior to the upgrade my installed version of Git,
which is included with Xcode or via the command
line developer tools, worked perfectly fine.
Now, post upgrade, I have some issues.
Let's check it out.
With my terminal open I still have the Git binary,
or at least a symlink on my system's path.
"which git",
which is located at "/usr/bin/git".
If Git is located at another location then you
aren't using the Apple provided Git binary.
Now, when I go to check the version of Git installed,
"git version", I get an error message like this.
I've encountered this error each year
that I performed the Mac OS upgrade.
By now my only surprise is that Apple
still hasn't fixed this issue.
All right, time for the solution.
It's basically reinstall the command
line developer tools with this command:
"sudo xcode-select  --install"
Actually the sudo part is optional;
Mac OS will simply prompt you for your credentials
before proceeding if you don't use sudo.
Now press enter to continue.
Now I'm prompted for my password, which is
required to run any command using sudo.
After that you'll be prompted to
either get Xcode, not now, or install.
Of course the middle option simply cancels this process.
The first option sends you to the Mac App Store
to download the latest version of Xcode,
which will include the command line developer tools.
The last option, install, will just install the
command line developer's tool part of the code suite.
That is the option I normally use, since I don't use Xcode
and there's no point in downloading a multi-gigabyte
application I'm not planning to use otherwise.
If you want to install everything, go right ahead.
You'll get Git and other tools updated that way as well.
For everyone else just click install to continue.
After that you'll be greeted with a license agreement dialog.
Feel free to read it if you so desire.
Otherwise continue by clicking on "Agree".
Now the installer will download and re-install
the latest copy of the command line developer
tools compatible with your system.
This will likely take several minutes, but I'll
speed things up with the magic of video editing.
Great. Once complete, click on the done button.
Once back to the terminal we should able to check
Git's version and not have an error this time.
"git version".
Awesome, it looks like we are back in business.
Well this really completes this video, but I
wanted to address one other topic while I'm here.
Sicne I'm certain someone will ask or pointed out
that's regarding alternative ways to install Git.
Yes it is true you can use package managers
like homebrew or MacPorts to install Git.
However they have their own issues, especially
if you do plan to use Xcode at any point.
This is why I generally recommend sticking with the flavor
of Git provided by Apple, it just keeps things simple.
If you already have your favorite package
manager and you never plan to use Xcode,
or at least you're comfortable overcoming the
conflicts, then you can certainly go that route.
You are likely to end up with a slightly newer version of Git,
but not enough to make a meaningful difference in daily work.

When given a choice between the SSL implementation
I like to stick with the open SSL library.
Click "Next" to continue.
This page is regarding how our line endings
for each of our files will be treated.
For the default setup for Windows we checkout
as Windows style and commit as Unix style.
That means any time you checkout a file, or do any editing,
Git will automatically change the line
endings to match the Windows system.
But, when it is time to commit,
it will commit as the Unix style,
which is the way that Git prefers to store any of it's files.
The last option is to checkout as is and to commit as-is;
basically we're not going to like Git do anything to our files.
And then the middle option, which is the
option I prefer, is to check out as-is,
that is regardless of how the line settings are
set up, and to commit as the Unix line endings.
That means eventually all my files that I
use with Git will use the Unix line ending.
This is a great option for cross-platform developers.
Click next to continue.
Regarding the terminal emulator I recommend using the MinTTY
Click next to continue.

--- Git Configuration ---

Welcome, in this video, we're going to set
up the minimal configuration for Git.
With the Git bash terminal open, let's
type the following Git commands.
The first command will set the username:
"git config --global user.name";
space; and then, in double quotes, your name.
The "--global" is actually important on this command,
because it will save this configuration item, that is the
item named "user.name", in the global configuration file.
That's actually a bit of a misnomer, because the
global configuration file is at the system user level.
That is, it will only be for my user account on this system.
Double check the command, and then press enter.
The next item we need to set is
actually the user's e-mail address.
"git config --global user.email"; space; and
then, in double quotes, your e-mail address.
Again the "--global" saves the "user.email" entry
to the global or user level configuration file.
Double check the line and then press enter.
Great, now let's list our available
global configuration settings.
"git config --global --list"
This will list all our settings at the
global level, or actually the user level.
Both of our entries are listed.
The global configuration file for Git,
that is the user level configuration file,
is stored in a special text file called ".gitconfig," that
is stored in the root directory of your user's profile.
Let's check that out now.
Let's use the "cat" command, which will
simply print out the contents of a file.
"cat", then we'll use the '~' character to
represent the user's home directory; "/.gitconfig"
You should be able to use tab completion.
Now press Enter.
This is the contents of our "gitconfig" file as of
right now, since we only have two global entries.

--- Java Configuration ---

Welcome, in this video, I'm going to set
up the Java Home environment variable.
Some programs that use Java require a special environment
variable named Java home to exist in order to run.
Before we can do that, we need to
make sure that Java is installed. To
verify that, just open the command
prompt and type "java -version".
If Java is installed, it will respond with
the version of Java that is currently
installed. Now that we've done that,
we can close our command prompt.
Let's go find where the Java JDK is installed.
So I'm going to open up my file explorer.
File Explorer is sometimes called Windows Explorer in prior
versions of Windows. I'm going to go to the c drive,
and program files, which is where 64 bit versions of
applications are installed on the 64 bit version of Windows.
Going under there, I can see that I have a Java folder;
going into there, I have the jdk folder and the jre folder.
For Java home, we want the JDK version. Once we've
made it into that folder, click on the path bar.
And that will switch to the traditional path to this folder.
Control+c or right click and copy that value.
Now, while in file explorer, right click
on "This PC", then go to properties.
Once the system window loads, go
"Advanced system settings". That
will pop up the system properties
dialogue on to the "Advanced" tab.
Go to the "Environment Variables..." button. Once you click
that, you will be on the environment variables dialogue.
I prefer creating the Java Home variable
as a System variable. So under the
"System variables" section, I'm going
to click on the "New..." button.
Once the new system variable dialogue loads, for "Variable
name:" type the value "JAVA_HOME", using all capital letters.
Then, go to the "Variable value:"
field, and press control+v or right
click and paste the value that we
just copied from file explorer.
Double check that value, and make sure it's
the path to the Java JDK. Now click on OK.
Back on the "Environment Variables" dialogue, click "OK".
And again, "OK" on the "System Properties" Dialogue.
Back at the "System" window, we can close that window.
And now it's safe to close the file explorer.
Alright, let's test this out. Go
to the command prompt; we can use
the set command to echo the value
of a given environment variable.
Type "set JAVA_HOME" again using all capital letters.
Double-check, and then press enter.
JAVA_HOME is equal to the path to the Java JDK.
That's exactly what we want.
Great, now that we've verified that,
we can close the command prompt.

Welcome, in this video, I'm going to walk through the steps

page loads, scroll down to the
available download options, and for
Windows we want the "Binary zip".
Go to the column for the link, and
click on the link. That downloads
the entire distribution for Apache
Maven, at least the binary version.
Once the .zip file has completed downloading,
expand our options and select "Show in folder".
That option should take us to our downloads
folder, with our Apache Maven zip file selected.
With that file selected, right click on it, and
then chose extract all from the pop-up menu.
That will launch an extraction wizard, with the
first page asking us where to extract the files.
I like to place all my development tools in a common area.
Which I call "DevTools" off the c-drive.
This can be any location you want on your system, including
other drives. You just need to remember where this is.
Once you've either selected or typed
in the destination, leave the "Show
extracted files when complete"
checked, and then click on "Extract".
Once the extraction is complete, the extraction
wizard will open up the destination folder.
The destination folder should contain a folder within
it named after Apache Maven plus the version number.
Go into that folder, and we just need to make sure
that we have everything set up the way we need to.
While I'm here, I'm going to select the address bar,
which will normalize to a normal path to this folder.
Then control+c, or right click and copy, then, within file
explorer, right click on "This PC", then go to "Properties".
Once the "System" window loads, go
to "Advanced system settings". That
launches the "System Properties"
dialogue, on the "Advanced" tab.
At the bottom of that tab, click on the
"Environment Variables..." button. Once there,
we're going to create a new system variable
and edit the existing path variable.
Under, "System Variables" click on the "New..." button. For
the variable name type M2_HOME in all capital letters.
Then, in the "Variable Value:" paste in the value that
of the path to Maven, that we copied in the prior steps.
Control+v or right click, paste. Double
check the value and then click on "OK".
Now, within the system variables,
scroll down until you find the "path"
variable. Great, select the path variable,
then click on the "Edit..." button.
On the "Variable value:" go to the end of the value. You can
use the keyboard key "End" to go to the very end of the value.
Now, separate the path with a semicolon. Now, instead of
pasting in the path like we did in the previous step,
we're going to use the environment
variable that we just created. To
reference another environment variable,
we surround it with percent signs.
So %%, then jump back between the two, and let's
type in our environment variable: M2_Home.
Now let's advance the last percent, and tack on \bin. That
will tell the path variable to include the bin directory
underneath the location to M2_HOME. That's the actual
path to all the executables that are bundled with Maven.
Once we've done that, double check
our value, then click on "OK".
Once back to the environment
variables dialogue, click on "OK".
Again, on the "System properties"
dialogue, click on "OK". Now
that we're back to the "System"
window, we can close this window.
And, at this point, it's also safe
to close file explorer; and while
we're at it, let's close our browser.

work equally as well with Git
Bash or the Windows PowerShell.

mvn, space, -version. Then press enter.

version number and some other
information, then you know that
Maven has been installed correctly.
Once we've validated that, we can close the command prompt.

--- Jenkins Installation on Windows ---

In this video, we are going to download
and install Jenkins for Windows 10.
While there are other approaches, like putting
Jenkins in a virtual machine or a docker container,
running Jenkins directly on your system is
the fastest way to get to know Jenkins.
Let's start off by opening your favorite
web browser and go to http://jenkins.io.
Once the page loads, find and click on the Download button.
Right now, that pops up a couple choices;
the Long Term Support (or LTS) release
or the *Weekly* Release. If you are more
adventurous, you can try the weekly release.
For now, let's stick with the LTS release
since that is likely to be more stable.
Click on the down-arrow and select **Windows**
to download the Jenkins installer archive
specifically for our system. Once the
installer package has finished downloading,
click on the archive to open it and then run the program
contained within it to run the *Setup Wizard* for Jenkins.
On the Welcome page, doesn't have much
on it, so click Next to keep going.
For the Destination Folder, you can choose another
location, but the default works well for me.
Click Next. Now, click the Install button for
the setup wizard to start installing Jenkins.
You may be prompted to confirm you want to
install this software; click Yes to continue.
At the end, click Finish to close the installer.
Doing so, the installer will attempt
to launch the Jenkins homepage,
which may prompt you to choose a web browser to use for this.
Your web browser should open to localhost on port 8080.

--- Jenkins Getting Started ---

(English captions)
Either the first time you access
Jenkins at http://localhost:8080,
or immediately after clicking the
Finish button from the install wizard,
Jenkins will guide you through a getting started wizard
to further customize Jenkins before getting started.
On the first page, you will be given
instructions on how to Unlock Jenkins.
In my case, a specific, cryptic password
has been written to a text file
within the Jenkins installation folder on my system.
Simply copy the full path to that file and then
use your favorite text editor to open that file.
Copy the password text in that file and then paste the value
into the Administrator password field on this screen.
Next, we are prompted to customize Jenkins via plugins.
We will cover plugins in more detail later in the course.
For now, just click the Install
suggested plugins to get started.
Jenkins will automatically install and configure
the standard or recommended set of plugins,
along with any required dependencies. After that,
Jenkins will prompt us to create an admin account.
We will use this account most of the time initial
until we get to the security section of this course.
I'm going to call the username admin,
provide a password and then confirm it.
Use Admin Account for full name and
finally provide an email address.
Don't just make up an email address for this admin user;
you may need access to this email address later.
Once done, click Save and Finish
and then Start using Jenkins. Now, you
should be on the main page of Jenkins.

--- Starting and Stopping Jenkins via Windows Services ---

(English captions)
When we installed Jenkins, it was
installed as a Windows system service
which means the program is always running, and
will start again if we reboot our system.
Under the hood, Jenkins runs on top
of a small web server called Jetty
which is a light-weight Java web application server.
I mention that, because Jenkins won't
stop running when you close your browser.
However, we can tweak the URL in the browser to tell
Jenkins to restart or simply reload its configuration.
Make sure Jenkins is open in your browser,
then go to your browser's address bar,
and remove any trailing information
after the localhost and port number.
Then type slash restart and then press enter.
Jenkins will likely prompt you to
confirm if you really want to restart.
Go ahead, let's move forward with restarting Jenkins.
This restart URL trick
is handy to remember when you want to restart
Jenkins when Jenkins seems to need it.
When Jenkins refreshes, you might be
prompted to log back in to Jenkins again,
or you might end up on the Jenkins dashboard.
We can also restart Jenkins using the Windows service.
To do that, right-click on the Start
Menu and select Computer Management.
Once the Computer Management system
loads, expand Services and Applications,
and then click on Services. This will list all
the services currently setup on your system.
Let's orient our service panel and
browser window so we can see both.
Now, find the item named Jenkins and click on it to select it.
You can either click the Stop or Restart
links or right-click on the Jenkins item
to bring up the service pop-up menu and then
select Stop or Restart. Let's try out stop first.
After the service has been stopped, jump back to
your web browser and refresh or reload the page.
Your web browser should give you an error
message, telling you the page cannot be found.
The exact message will depend on your browser,
but the behavior is basically the same.
Back on the service panel, since
the Jenkins service has stopped,
you will have access to the Start link or
the Start item on the service pop-up menu.
Let's start things up again, click Start. Now,
in a few seconds, refresh the web browser again.
This time, you will either be meet with a page
from Jenkins telling you Jenkins is in the
process of starting up, or you will already be
back on the login page or Jenkins dashboard.
In any case, back on the service panel, you will
see the Jenkins service has started once again.
The restart menu item or link will stop Jenkins
and then immediately start it back again.
While you shouldn't have to start, stop or restart
Jenkins using the Windows service very often,
there are times when this is the only choice left.
